## Part opening

<!-- pedagogy-unit: part:OBSERVATIONS::part-opening -->

Part ID: `part:OBSERVATIONS`

Purpose:

> The next studies connect neural activity to eye control in monkeys and to location in rodents and bats. Chapters 7–9 follow the path from a neural command through eye muscles to measured movement, and test a possible source of drift. Chapters 10–12 explain where hippocampal nerve cells fire as an animal moves and compare a spatial model with recordings. Chapter 13 asks what these findings establish together and which connections remain untested.

## ch:EYE-DRIFT - Eye drift points to a shared neural drive

Source file: `content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md`

Purpose: Even when a monkey looks at a stationary spot, its eyes drift slightly. This chapter explains how paired measurements of eye angle and the firing of neurons that command eye muscles support a shared neural source of that drift, before considering a particular circuit mechanism.

Prerequisites: ch:PERSISTENCE, ch:MEMORY-DYNAMICS

Sidecar sections: From stored eye position to muscle action, The recordings pair one motor neuron with movement at a time, A fitted model links movement to firing rate, A calibrated filter predicts eye position from one cell's spikes, The measured correlation challenges independent motor noise alone, Calibrated covariance estimates the common share of movement variance

Blocks: 6 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::ch:EYE-DRIFT -->

### Chapter opening

Location: line 1, anchor `ch:EYE-DRIFT`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 3
Closing passage line: 7

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p3 sha256 0068733666c266ddb78ca7386ae1c14b55f3cc36b7e9d73bdf3435eea8bcbaa4 -->
p3, lines 3-3; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p5

> Looking steadily at a small spot does not hold the eye perfectly still. [Between brief, rapid movements called [**microsaccades**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, the eye makes a small, smooth, irregular movement called fixational drift.]{.definition-explanation} [Does this movement begin as noise in the muscles, or does the brain send a changing command to them?]{.key-statement} Ben-Shushan and colleagues answer this in two stages. Their recordings support a large shared drive upstream of the motor neurons. Their model then proposes a particular source: random motion of the eye position stored in a memory circuit.[^p2-question]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p5 sha256 ca0bc9dead0d4e995e3e496d098a0a1213854eccf928570a5bf5ff1473f4f55a -->
p5, lines 5-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p7

> This distinction connects the previous chapters to a measurement outside the brain. A memory coordinate can diffuse inside a neural network, but the movement produced by that coordinate also depends on the muscles and the mechanics of the eye. We need to follow the signal through those stages before comparing a neural diffusion law with an eye trajectory.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 3240811d7c0d01b6d80c1c03bc1ac671c9735238e431fb6625d93370d9d8c698 -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p11

> A recording from one motor neuron becomes useful for this comparison only after its spikes and the eye's movement can be expressed on the same scale. The larger movements provide that calibration. Applying it during fixation then lets us ask whether independent motor noise could explain the observed relationship, and estimate the shared contribution under explicit gain and independence assumptions.

### From stored eye position to muscle action

Location: line 9, anchor `sec:EYE-PATHWAY`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 11
Closing passage line: 30

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p11 sha256 650c22648e27fafc78f9297e562cc3f94135cc258ea8284a7043ab317a70aed6 -->
p11, lines 11-11; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p7; next: p13

> The eye needs an ongoing command to maintain a position. A brief command to move is therefore insufficient: after the movement, a circuit must maintain activity representing the new position. [The [**oculomotor integrator**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the neural circuit that performs this storage. “Integrator” refers to turning a movement command into a lasting position signal.]{.definition-explanation} In the proposed mechanism, the continuous attractor described in the [foundations](ch-persistence.html#sec-foundations-attractor) and [network model](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-spikes) stores horizontal eye position. Noise moves the stored value along that attractor.[^p2-pathway]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p13 sha256 5995a87b00a2781baaf082d1452dae3c172e39e017de07508448fd744d1da3b5 -->
p13, lines 13-13; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p11; next: p15

> [The signal reaches [**ocular motoneurons (OMNs)**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, neurons whose spikes activate the eye muscles.]{.definition-explanation} [The recordings came from the [**abducens nucleus**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a group of brainstem neurons that includes OMNs activating the lateral rectus muscle, which turns the eye outward.]{.definition-explanation} Other neurons in that nucleus carry signals to other neurons rather than directly to muscle. The study separated these cell types using their eye movement response parameters. [Finally, the muscles and the mechanical properties of the eye, together called the [**ocular plant**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, turn neural activity into movement.]{.definition-explanation}[^p2-recordings]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p15 sha256 a24a2319d48bd386c94e7b85be54277c1ed845ddc8bd86d17b98b23ea85b6a20 -->
p15, lines 15-17; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p13; next: p21

p21, lines 21-22; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p15; next: p24

p24, lines 24-24; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p21; next: p26

> There are three possible contributions to an observed drift:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p26 sha256 62ac9efe5322aa0da540d474f94aaa95f5da3a9d44275a070fb362ebeea31c6d -->
p26, lines 26-26; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p24; next: p27

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p27 sha256 99f5fdcaccef21a06249954e75433916d06015b789624b3bc35cbaf79820bad1 -->
p27, lines 27-27; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p26; next: p28

> Private spike variability in individual OMNs.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p28 sha256 0249dc07c5304816448f61c79df07363b728ff66017cf18997a826c1434b65ee -->
p28, lines 28-28; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p27; next: p30

> Variability after those neurons, including muscle and measurement noise.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p30 sha256 bdee8fea92e19ef56386ad365c414c8c5e2f2e1523f0681e96bda69742a06302 -->
p30, lines 30-30; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p28; next: p34

> [Private spike fluctuations can partly cancel when many neurons act together. A common command survives this averaging.]{.key-statement} This is why the noisy activity of one neuron can reveal a source that dominates the much smoother eye movement.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::sec:EYE-RECORDINGS -->

### The recordings pair one motor neuron with movement at a time

Location: line 32, anchor `sec:EYE-RECORDINGS`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 34
Closing passage line: 57

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p34 sha256 8387c951e1856d2f21296797409b1dfc28b43d833e6da1c5f0f0da89055a5ef2 -->
p34, lines 34-34; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p30; next: p36

> The authors reanalyzed earlier experiments in two male rhesus macaques. With their heads held still, the monkeys first looked at a stationary spot of light on a monitor and then followed the moving spot. A wire search coil on one eye measured the eye's angle while an electrode recorded one neuron's spikes. There were 57 identified OMNs. [Each recording paired one OMN with eye movement; there were no simultaneous recordings of multiple OMNs. Thus the analysis cannot directly measure the covariance between two recorded motor neurons.]{.key-statement}[^p2-recordings][^p2-single]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p36 sha256 2619e69fdb09ac2d7d66b5c6495d49fb63dd6924a2b866b628207d75ff7cd44d -->
p36, lines 36-36; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p34; next: p38

> The initial stationary target was followed by 500–700 ms of required fixation before the target stepped and began moving. Neural voltage was sampled at 25 kHz and eye position at 1 kHz. The authors detected microsaccades using eye speed and acceleration, removed 30 ms on each side of them, and manually checked the remaining segments. For the main displacement analysis, segments shorter than 350 ms were excluded and longer ones were split into nonoverlapping 350 ms pieces. There were 302 such pieces per cell on average, with different counts across cells. These repeated observations improve estimates within two animals; they do not create 57 independent animal samples.[^p2-segments]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p38 sha256 715a3b40870e606d42e18c6be920744cf67fde4198516b64afa3596f75d3d8c5 -->
p38, lines 38-40; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p36; next: p44

> <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure"> <summary>Original paper figure: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), Extended Data Figure 7</summary> <div class="publisher-fold-body">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p44 sha256 fcd1e43c0c40c2d1dd2932edba0594c38927752553522ce5ed959562b6a78f49 -->
p44, lines 44-45; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p38; next: p47

> </div> </details>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p47 sha256 a5553c8b5555c7a82e678aaa235473290af1992e87fb9da54586d7c3f3ecbfba -->
p47, lines 47-47; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p44; next: p49

> The large movements have a useful role even though the scientific question concerns tiny drifts. They produce enough rate change to estimate how each cell's activity depends on eye movement. The fitted relationship is then applied during fixation. This transfer is an assumption to examine, because an incorrect gain during drift would change the inferred common variance. [[**Gain**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the scale that converts a change in one signal into a change in the other.]{.definition-explanation}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p49 sha256 2455077ea873544e8751a359f556bad9e2360dcfd3d084fb56fe958abd535cf2 -->
p49, lines 49-49; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p47; next: p51

> The diagram separates the physical pathway from the paired recordings and the later calculation of predicted eye position.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p51 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p51, lines 51-51; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p49; next: p55

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p55 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p55, lines 55-55; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p51; next: p57

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p57 sha256 33adabde654e5601e7e317575a762b5ecae78bae70b3b4eccf75adc46aaceb9d -->
p57, lines 57-57; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p55; next: p61

> The upper path describes a possible physical source and its output. The recording design provides one cell and eye movement at a time; it does not measure covariance between simultaneously recorded motor neurons. The predicted position is a later analysis result, not a signal sent backward through the physical pathway.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::sec:EYE-FORWARD-FIT -->

### A fitted model links movement to firing rate

Location: line 59, anchor `sec:EYE-FORWARD-FIT`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 61
Closing passage line: 97

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p61 sha256 2d5a4bd1401864e33fc9fc4990186090a957c2651f19cbb7b6cbd5e46eb26288 -->
p61, lines 61-61; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p57; next: p68

> Position alone does not predict a motor neuron's activity during a movement. At the same position, firing can differ when the eye is moving or accelerating. The paper therefore fits

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p68 sha256 35fbef1321d69097d5535fe106bd53ca030f8ada3646621fe938d6269e80e804 -->
p68, lines 68-68; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p61; next: p76

> Here $E$ is horizontal eye position in degrees and $\widehat f$ is predicted firing rate in spikes/s. The position threshold $E_T$ sets where the position term reaches zero. The coefficients have units

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p76 sha256 b52ef5bb23ab58b6ff244096877ca8c54f7bddf411f43ec8fcfa4a81709db446 -->
p76, lines 76-76; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p68; next: p78

> Multiplying by position, velocity, and acceleration gives spikes/s in all three terms.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p78 sha256 2f0cfe2b999f5cf1596f2e748098edfabf2ff080eae22fc7651fd73fcc536220 -->
p78, lines 78-78; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p76; next: p80

> The position term describes ongoing firing associated with holding the eye at an angle. The velocity term allows a different rate while the eye passes through that same angle; the acceleration term allows another change while its velocity changes. Thus $k$, $r$, and $m$ describe different demands on the motor signal. They are fitted coefficients of the combined neural and mechanical relationship. In particular, $m$ is not a direct measurement of the eye's mass. The delay $\Delta t$ is in seconds: firing at time $t$ predicts movement at the later time $t+\Delta t$ for a positive neural lead. This is the paper's Eq. 2, including its threshold and time shift.[^p2-forward]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p80 sha256 f3fc314e12bf31a6fd59e7b34dfb99e911184c925b34e79a5945cd1023947062 -->
p80, lines 80-80; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p78; next: p87

> The observed rate $f(t)$ was estimated as the inverse of the interval between successive spikes, constant between those spikes. For each cell, the authors minimized the sum of squared rate errors,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p87 sha256 c36d17f381179b57d22c89b867c01ec25faa88683b6c1562b24179d0ee188176 -->
p87, lines 87-87; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p80; next: p89

> $a$ labels trials and $T_a$ is trial duration. This is Eq. 3 with the trial count renamed so that it cannot be confused with a neuron count. Fits used full trials, including fixation and pursuit. To predict a trial's rate, that trial was left out of the parameter fit. Acceleration was estimated from velocity after local polynomial smoothing over 21 ms. Trials in which the rate fell below 20 Hz were discarded: the linear formula is intended for active cells above firing threshold, whereas firing cannot become negative.[^p2-forward]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p89 sha256 8aca25e2b1096d955ee6c31aad4214d98e204ec98de20c0201df33e98bac3816 -->
p89, lines 89-89; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p87; next: p91

> During fixation, the authors subtracted each segment's mean firing rate, joined the segments, and compared observed and predicted rate fluctuations. Figure 1c illustrates a weak relationship for one cell; Fig. 1d shows a positive population effect. The one sided test asks whether the mean correlation is positive, compared with a zero-mean reference. [Its [**p value**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the probability, under that reference, of a test statistic at least as large as observed.]{.definition-explanation} Across 57 cells it gave $p=3\times10^{-9}$. A weak single cell relationship is expected here because the tiny movement produces small rate changes against much larger spike variability. This is the first evidence of a relationship, before any estimate of the fraction of common motion.[^p2-rate-result]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p91 sha256 7bdb311511f99b82f006e27389a713c838c70b56d25ed0e0a9c1d8ec53d11e76 -->
p91, lines 91-93; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p89; next: p97

> <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure"> <summary>Original paper figure: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), Figure 1</summary> <div class="publisher-fold-body">

p97, lines 97-98; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p91; next: p102

### A calibrated filter predicts eye position from one cell's spikes

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p102 sha256 70dc4869b589bdcb1411f4aa4e031d924439531945bdff04fad85b4da0f4c0fe -->
p102, lines 102-102; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p97; next: p104

> To ask how much eye movement is shared with a neuron, it helps to express both signals in degrees. [The paper reverses the fitted relationship: a cell's spike train becomes the input, and its prediction of eye position becomes the output. This operation is an inverse of the movement-to-rate fit, rather than a new fit to small drift residuals.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p104 sha256 4f6c7d464c5f055b961eb1cbfceab1493dc76af31b519d8f4014631f024aa40f -->

> [A [**causal filter**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} computes its present output from present and past input. For the equation above, its [**impulse response**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} $h_0(u)$ is the position response to one instantaneous spike at time zero.]{.definition-explanation} To factor the equation, consider a time component $e^{pt}$: differentiation multiplies it by $p$. The algebraic ratio of output to input for that component is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p117 sha256 19dbd09a54232a01ba1f66b8e50cd5c1f1d56443af3ae6383d94723a2d4a446f -->

> Factoring the quadratic gives the slow and fast decay times

p124, lines 124-124; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p117; next: p126

> These are the paired signs in the paper's Eq. 7, with $\tau_+>\tau_-$ when $k,r,m>0$ and $r^2>4km$. Under these conditions both components decay without oscillation.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p126 sha256 3248b6d2744bf05c8ea246a86fe27cbe19876d1e959b6c31a3ae60fc06fa5bfd -->
p126, lines 126-126; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p124; next: p133

> At rest, the inverse equation gives $x=f/k$: with the threshold fixed, doubling $k$ halves the position offset predicted from the same steady firing rate. During a change, $r$ and $m$ also affect the response through the two timescales. This is a consequence of the fitted relationship, not evidence that changing one recorded neuron's rate alone sets eye position. The same factorization gives two useful identities:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p133 sha256 a2ac4d749ee4165fb46a31c375d552a55ed758a66b963b8690e4d5da22e3b600 -->
p133, lines 133-133; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p126; next: p141

> The corresponding impulse response is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p141 sha256 17b1baaff251def1ac150a79aebb365830c810003d113f5b384376630eed77b0 -->
p141, lines 141-141; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p133; next: p148

> $\Theta(u)$ is zero for $u<0$ and one for $u>0$; it imposes causality. Restoring the fitted neural lead delays this response: $h(u)=h_0(u-\Delta t)$. Write the spike train as $\xi(t)=\sum_j\delta(t-t_j)$, where $\delta$ is an ideal instantaneous impulse of unit area, so integration over one spike counts one event. The prediction is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p148 sha256 b19de659cfff4be0d07f867518e8124ed0b4323c9bff564d09b5864c9daafe62 -->
p148, lines 148-148; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p141; next: p152

> These expressions reproduce Eqs. 4–6, including the factor $\tau_+\tau_-/m$ and the delay. [The [**convolution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} integral adds the responses to all past spikes.]{.definition-explanation} With spike count treated as a count, $\xi$ has units spikes/s and $h$ has units degrees/spike. Each spike contributes a decaying position response; adding those responses produces degrees.[^p2-inversion]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p152 sha256 4eec0db35e0cd5097e595acded14851f798b0df8940c381d3a89cfa31f16d34f -->
p152, lines 152-152; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p148; next: p158

> We can obtain the response to a spike directly from the differential equation. After the spike, the input is zero, and each of the two exponentials solves the remaining homogeneous equation. Write their combination as

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p158 sha256 d5a34ccb8a05bed2a1a5184b92b28398d972bd5b9f8f5e276f2e8d2167d6b11f -->
p158, lines 158-158; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p152; next: p165

> To find $A$ and $B$, treat the spike as a short pulse of unit area and shrink its duration to zero. The position change during that pulse tends to zero, so $h_0(0^+)=0$. Integrating $m h_0''+r h_0'+k h_0$ across the pulse gives $m h_0'(0^+)=1$: the velocity changes by $1/m$, while position and velocity before the causal response are zero. Therefore

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p165 sha256 cf9badc5a588635909f7997060bc4015fc6ee7aaada1d42cb3a7fbf0a4ce583a -->
p165, lines 165-165; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p158; next: p169

> Substituting $B=-A$ gives $A(1/\tau_- -1/\tau_+)=1/m$, hence $A=\tau_+\tau_-/[m(\tau_+-\tau_-)]$. Making the response zero before the spike gives the impulse response $h_0(u)$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p169 sha256 9b2ee830691f3039bde3143aa4ee58dbc946248e991762c153bd2baf666366b1 -->
p169, lines 169-169; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p165; next: p176

> For a constructed example, take $k=4$ spikes/(s deg), $\tau_+=0.120$ s, and $\tau_-=0.005$ s. The identities above require $r=0.500$ spikes/deg and $m=0.00240$ spikes s/deg. Then

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p176 sha256 8c7bd8b13dc15819406aa2a41498f782c06fdd5848a2979e41a963c86d646976 -->
p176, lines 176-176; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p169; next: p180

> and its area is $2.174(0.120-0.005)=0.250$ deg s/spike, equal to $1/k$. A steady rate of 80 spikes/s predicts an offset of 20 degrees above $E_T$. This checks the gain as well as the time constants; it is not a fit to a recorded cell.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p180 sha256 1682b05d154bb130d709606004cc56a1752f384ec76339a5c9586211f2f1c302 -->
p180, lines 180-180; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p176; next: p188

> The kernel's area checks its normalization:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p188 sha256 9342257b323d29a7490feb94bbe753fbac3b7da45624d5c8c5781848515e0a92 -->
p188, lines 188-188; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p180; next: p192

> Thus a constant input rate $f_0$ predicts $E_T+f_0/k$, exactly the static forward relation. A kernel with unit area would give the wrong conversion unless $k$ had already been absorbed into the input. When the two decay times coincide, $r^2=4km$, the limit is $h_0(u)=\Theta(u)u e^{-u/\tau}/m$. The distinct exponential formula is used for the fitted responses in the paper.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p192 sha256 b1bf0bc1f9c4355b1d2ee3a5062e99af443a7304723d3423bfc3997268a43f41 -->
p192, lines 192-192; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p188; next: p194

> Figure 2a–b shows typical fitted times of about 5 ms and 120 ms. Figure 2c shows that a single cell's reconstructed drift is much noisier than the measured movement. Across 350 ms displacement pairs, the mean Pearson correlation was $\langle R\rangle=0.17\pm0.02$, [where the error is the [**standard error of the mean (SEM)**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. SEM estimates uncertainty in the across-cell mean, rather than the spread of individual cells or uncertainty across animals.]{.definition-explanation} The one sided test gave $p=3.8\times10^{-12}$. Shuffling which measured segment was paired with which spike prediction centered the comparison near zero. Here and below $\langle\cdot\rangle$ over cells means a population average.[^p2-correlation]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p194 sha256 e37d3efdb274776cdd45cd19e609f16f084c1d7502789455ffb986d1c787addc -->
p194, lines 194-196; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p192; next: p200

> <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure"> <summary>Original paper figure: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), Figure 2</summary> <div class="publisher-fold-body">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p200 sha256 fcd1e43c0c40c2d1dd2932edba0594c38927752553522ce5ed959562b6a78f49 -->
p200, lines 200-201; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p194; next: p205

> </div> </details>

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::sec:EYE-SHARED-VARIANCE -->

### The measured correlation challenges independent motor noise alone

Location: line 203, anchor `sec:EYE-SHARED-VARIANCE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 205
Closing passage line: 237

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p205 sha256 b54158d1b0264f8858ec0971143edd70bb5633c70c4b329e7ea088dd725c42bf -->
p205, lines 205-205; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p200; next: p207

> A correlation of 0.17 does not immediately tell us how much motion comes from the common command. First we can ask a more limited question: could all the motion come from independent motor neuron fluctuations?

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p207 sha256 7b91bf7f7698525cd5c8f1eb6f2895f170f6d4d45c4d9a54f438660a098f924f -->
p207, lines 207-207; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p205; next: p215

> For a centered displacement $Y$ and one centered prediction $X$, the best linear prediction $bX$ minimizes $\operatorname{Var}(Y-bX)$. Differentiating with respect to $b$ gives $b=\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)/\operatorname{Var}(X)$. Its explained fraction of variance is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p215 sha256 b59eaa31a873e36c591d28219fb63b3016401079cd2a68fc5699df4f031dc850 -->
p215, lines 215-215; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p207; next: p217

> [This is the [**coefficient of determination**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} for a linear prediction with a fitted gain.]{.definition-explanation} It does not assert that the raw, unscaled position predictor itself has a small squared error.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p217 sha256 19e045d8607cf3876e73fdbde10520406a5e677e00c0c12c4b2e1857a8d6b503 -->
p217, lines 217-217; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p215; next: p225

> Suppose there are $N_m$ independent single neuron predictors $X_i$. Standardize each to variance one and also standardize $Y$. Independence makes the $X_i$ uncorrelated, so the residual after using them all has variance

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p225 sha256 87bd59ca3ad47cbbf9f483a691c623d8d48f9f9801046f709ee61ad1b5739a64 -->
p225, lines 225-225; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p217; next: p227

> Consequently $\sum_iR_i^2\le1$, and an average neuron explains at most $1/N_m$. This proves the bound used in Supplementary Eq. S1. It does not require equal muscle effects from all neurons. Applying it to the sampled cells does require their average explanatory power to represent the relevant motor population.[^p2-independent-bound]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p227 sha256 0514e747eaf71b383c2e16919c381e3a8ef595ae2542aeaa531c338157ab6990 -->
p227, lines 227-227; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p225; next: p235

> The authors avoid directly averaging noisy squared correlations. Instead,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p235 sha256 b76109fd76b140aa63a585ef0d6101193551e329af5506816a08bd74d9e82520 -->
p235, lines 235-235; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p227; next: p237

> [Thus the estimated mean correlation implies an average explained fraction of at least about 2.9%. For 1,000 independently contributing motor neurons, the bound would be 0.1%; for several thousand it is smaller. The mismatch argues against independent OMN noise as the sole source.]{.key-statement} Shared fluctuations can allow many cells to explain the same part of the movement, so their explained fractions can overlap.[^p2-independent-bound]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p237 sha256 56567703098d6aaa1d1e145327c006422918468b8966daa0ac8fff9abcc8dcc9 -->
p237, lines 237-237; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p235; next: p241

> This step finds shared variation. Locating its source upstream also uses a biological premise: the private variability generated within OMNs is taken to be independent across cells. The paper supports that premise using anatomical and stimulation studies indicating little local recurrent coupling in the relevant motor nuclei. It was not tested by simultaneous recordings in this data set. Common inputs can correlate the total activity of OMNs even when their privately generated spike noise is independent.[^p2-independence]

### Calibrated covariance estimates the common share of movement variance

Location: line 239, anchor `sec:EYE-CHI`

Opening passage line: 241

p251, lines 251-251; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p250; next: p253

p262, lines 262-262; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p253; next: p269

p269, lines 269-269; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p262; next: p271

> These are Eqs. 11–15 in displacement form. The gain assumption requires that the relation calibrated during larger movements still applies during drift. There is also a small covariance between $\epsilon$ and $\eta$, because the recorded neuron's private spikes contribute both to its predictor and to actual eye motion. The paper neglects this contribution as order $1/N_m$ under its many neuron model.[^p2-chi-method]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p271 sha256 01e7757b7ffe43dfc2273cd2895457e71bb691dd44694c85207a8e7d89aa11eb -->
p271, lines 271-271; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p269; next: p279

> Correlation divides the same covariance by a different denominator:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p279 sha256 0c8f50d52233fddaaa2a6534b98cb0afbc096e88762d93eb3fd0d87dd236af82 -->
p279, lines 279-279; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p271; next: p281

> [Large private noise in a cell inflates $\operatorname{Var}(X)$ and makes $R$ small. It need not make the common contribution to the real movement small, because many independent private contributions average down there.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p281 sha256 8e19185c156210567a045f638d7d3d1269765f40e35d9cff42cebc457898c408 -->
p281, lines 281-281; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p279; next: p283

p283, lines 283-283; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p281; next: p285

p285, lines 285-285; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p283; next: p293

> These numbers are constructed to expose the distinction, not extracted from individual cells. Take independent centered variables with

p293, lines 293-293; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p285; next: p300

> Then $\operatorname{Var}(Y)=0.01000$, $\operatorname{Var}(X)=0.23266$, and $\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)=0.00820$, all in deg$^2$. It follows that

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p300 sha256 340326265819a30fbab12ba07ada323a51f6ce430f6a5f60f91ca86cba3efc4f -->
p300, lines 300-300; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p293; next: p304

> One cell explains $R^2\simeq2.89\%$ of displacement variance after linear rescaling, while the common signal supplies 82% of actual displacement variance. The larger, poorly predicted part of the single cell signal is private prediction noise.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p304 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p304, lines 304-304; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p300; next: p308

p308, lines 308-308; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p304; next: p310

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p310 sha256 7092966ea3b73970b3202c2bb54e21f73ef4164d966c5c6ccb2b808501e1b297 -->
p310, lines 310-310; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p308; next: p312

> Private prediction noise enlarges the denominator of R without changing the covariance. The bars use the same physical scale, so the equal common pieces have equal lengths. See [Shared and private variability, Eqs. 11–15, p. 10](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-fixational-drift.pdf#page=10).

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p312 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p312, lines 312-312; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p310; next: p314

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p314 sha256 a65b6fdc44c554175747a64e282a650477b18593ddf8eb1d86a93d5a0618d3a5 -->
p314, lines 314-314; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p312; next: p322

> In the experiment, each cell gives a noisy estimate $\chi_i$, and those estimates can lie outside $[0,1]$. The underlying variance fraction remains in that interval under the model, but the sample covariance need not. The paper estimates each standard error $\Delta\chi_i$ mainly from covariance uncertainty, treating the measured displacement variance in the denominator as comparatively well determined. It then uses inverse variance weighting:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p322 sha256 876497ff511a12c665439a2c6986da16e33ffca8e3a0bd9479ad7c09ce0ccf28 -->
p322, lines 322-322; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p314; next: none

> To see the weighting, let normalized weights $a_i$ sum to one. Independent errors give variance $\sum_i a_i^2(\Delta\chi_i)^2$ for $\sum_i a_i\chi_i$. Minimizing this subject to $\sum_i a_i=1$ gives $a_i\propto1/(\Delta\chi_i)^2$ and the error above. The paper additionally assumes approximately normal errors around a common value. The last expression is a propagated estimation error under those assumptions. It does not measure variation across animals or uncertainty in the gain and independence assumptions. [The reported result was $0.82\pm0.07$, a weighted mean and weighted error for horizontal displacement over 350 ms. It is neither a fraction of distance traveled nor the fraction that one neuron's spikes accurately predict.]{.key-statement}[^p2-chi-error]

## ch:EYE-TIMING - Drift growth distinguishes diffusion from filtered white noise

Source file: `content/chapters/04b-eye-timing.md`

Purpose: Eye movements recorded during steady gaze in monkeys look different over short and long time intervals. This chapter uses their timing to distinguish fluctuations around a fixed command from accumulated changes in the command, while accounting for eye-position measurement error.

Prerequisites: ch:EYE-DRIFT

Sidecar sections: Independent measurement error adds a constant offset to the MSD, The MSD plateaus for filtered white noise but grows for filtered diffusion

Blocks: 4 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p3 sha256 2ada4892d1bae18dbb4a7fb0daa280b06599104db9e02b261446d5f0d16cbd46 -->
p3, lines 3-3; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p5

> The shared movement estimate concerns a single interval length. [It leaves open whether the command merely fluctuates around a stable value or keeps accumulating changes. Comparing displacements over different time separations can distinguish these possibilities, but the smallest measured displacements may include a large contribution from recording error.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p5 sha256 67f5d8a6a1a82ef6848a40693e7df754d2e0adcd101947315a06c81f7341df5e -->
p5, lines 5-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p9

> The mean squared displacement measures the average squared position change over a chosen interval. Separating the recording error from that quantity is necessary before interpreting its growth. To test whether the command accumulates changes, we compare two possible inputs after a decaying muscle response: fluctuations that do not accumulate and a position that wanders by diffusion. Their different long interval behavior explains how a smooth eye movement can still reflect a wandering stored value.

### Independent measurement error adds a constant offset to the MSD

Location: line 7, anchor `sec:EYE-MEASUREMENT-NOISE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 9
Closing passage line: 58

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p9 sha256 90b024b2aaaf61ea685d07c0fd93c6ee94b4196b59c95b96526816f1ef60d77d -->
p9, lines 9-9; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p15

> The common variance calculation uses one interval length. To learn what kind of process drives motion, the authors next compare displacements at many time separations. Using the [earlier definition of mean squared displacement](ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-covariance),

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p15 sha256 61e04c7a4d88eae14f10f9fb9dfbcc2d3fea58e34f6077a8eabf1ac7f5d59b63 -->
p15, lines 15-15; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p9; next: p17

> is the mean squared displacement, or MSD, at lag $\ell$. The data estimate it within each microsaccade free segment, then average the available segment estimates at each lag. Larger lags have fewer eligible segments. The 1 ms sampling sets the available lag spacing.[^p2-msd]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p17 sha256 152949ab575328d32f2822b44acf9df72b8bacd3d378a2aad5fdc3895894c14c -->
p17, lines 17-17; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p15; next: p19

> At short lags, the displacement can be smaller than the search coil's measurement error. Suppose its reading is $E_{\rm obs}(t)=E(t)+\nu(t)$. The measurement error is assumed to have:

p23, lines 23-23; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p21; next: p35

> For a positive lag,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p35 sha256 e0d20ca4b4a0a1461ac3cdbe79c18120c7eab1f23e8b468e4f451040785f920f -->
p35, lines 35-35; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p23; next: p37

> The mixed movement-error term vanishes by independence. This is Eqs. 18–19. The correction is the variance of a difference of two errors, $2\sigma_m^2$, rather than the variance of a single position error. At exactly zero lag the same error is subtracted from itself, so the MSD is zero. A positive lag plateau near zero lag can still occur when successive measurement errors are independent.[^p2-msd-noise]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p37 sha256 91eb354b33542859a5ef799d21a5a128536d39012e8c71a56c78904f6fd5c5d4 -->
p37, lines 37-37; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p35; next: p39

> The authors estimated the plateau contribution at order $10^{-3}$ deg$^2$, checked its scale with a coil fixed in space, and subtracted that contribution from the observed MSD. [This is a correction of an average statistic, not knowledge of each trial's unknown noise.]{.key-statement} Smoothing the eye trajectories instead would also change the movement statistics the analysis seeks to measure. If the plateau estimate has uncertainty $10^{-4}$ deg$^2$, a corrected MSD of that size is unreliable even when the plateau itself is much larger. The peer review response uses this example to explain the shortest lag limitation.[^p2-noise-limit]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p39 sha256 89627107c32192e51784de1d71a40b67fe0be268a5c7e386a641b13bccb5f57e -->

p45, lines 45-46; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p39; next: p48

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p48 sha256 3d3ef08daebdef4d2f9c40989a971e5f939a96f451bf400685ca9fc0edd83f3d -->
p48, lines 48-48; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p45; next: p50

> For instance, a constructed measured MSD of $0.0014$ deg$^2$ with an estimated noise contribution $0.0010$ deg$^2$ leaves $0.0004$ deg$^2$. A $0.0001$ deg$^2$ uncertainty in the subtraction is already 25% of that result. This is why a corrected short lag point needs an uncertainty assessment as well as a positive value. If measurement errors were temporally correlated, the correction would instead be $2[\sigma_m^2-C_\nu(\ell)]$, where $C_\nu$ is their covariance at lag $\ell$; a constant subtraction would then need revision.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p50 sha256 b34ca6facaa856c70d89c10707022b3451e9117295904d69214d8b204a9b611d -->

The Brownian movement is chosen only to make the constant offset visible. See [Measurement noise correction, Eqs. 18–19, p. 10](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-fixational-drift.pdf#page=10).

### The MSD plateaus for filtered white noise but grows for filtered diffusion

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p62 sha256 0101d6c0734265a938f788bc99e58c63c0a11379abe76b55f0ca8fe03f35f881 -->

> The corrected MSD keeps increasing over the observed range, toward a second, while its logarithmic slope decreases and remains between one and two. Define that local slope by

> Ordinary diffusion has $M=2D\ell$ and $\alpha=1$. A roughly constant velocity over a short interval gives displacement $v\ell$ and $M=\mathbb E[v^2]\ell^2$, so $\alpha=2$. [Growth faster than linear is called [**superdiffusion**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. Here it describes a finite range of lags; it does not assert a single power law for arbitrarily long times.]{.definition-explanation}[^p2-msd-result]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p71 sha256 a2df773f3a69858b8070e30557fede51331a227ff7863be0191f3b794ee357ef -->

> Could temporally uncorrelated motor noise acquire the observed MSD just by passing through muscles? [A [**low pass filter**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} suppresses rapid input changes relative to slow ones.]{.definition-explanation} [[**White noise**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} here is an ideal zero mean input whose fluctuations at different times are uncorrelated. In continuous time its strength is defined by an intensity multiplying a delta function, rather than a finite variance at an individual time.]{.definition-explanation} [A stable, decaying response to this noise produces a [**stationary process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} in the sense needed here: its mean is constant and its covariance depends only on time separation.]{.definition-explanation} Its position variance consequently does not grow with absolute time. Let $E=h*\xi$, where

p99, lines 99-99; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p92; next: p101

> This simple filter has a short lag MSD proportional to $\ell$ and a long lag plateau. A double exponential filter is smoother at short lags, but still gives a plateau because its kernel also decays. Increasing $A$ raises the curve without removing its flattening. The paper's simulated private OMN noise, passed through muscle dynamics, is both too small and too flat at long lags. In Fig. 3b, using 2,000 heterogeneous OMNs, its contribution is about 1% of the observed variance near 900 ms.[^p2-msd-result]

p110, lines 110-110; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p107; next: p112

> Filtered white noise cannot explain continued long-lag growth. Suppose instead the input is a stored position $B(t)$ undergoing Brownian motion with $\mathbb E[(B(t+\ell)-B(t))^2]=2D\ell$. Its position variance accumulates. A low pass filter with nonzero static gain smooths the path while retaining that accumulating long time displacement variance. The following one exponential example is a teaching reconstruction, not the paper's full muscle model. It derives how smooth movement can have a slope between one and two even though the stored position undergoes ordinary diffusion.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p112 sha256 e947a3d4f4a1b23611f8aa204b2bb149785cfb02f5ccc687d1eb10b059e0d5aa -->
p112, lines 112-112; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p110; next: p118

> Let

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p118 sha256 2f80d95493c1d424754d113c2b4bfe8df26ceda6ca85e632de6391b89251bd56 -->
p118, lines 118-118; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p112; next: p120

> Here $B$ is the changing position command and $E$ is the eye angle that follows it, both in degrees. The mismatch $B-E$ drives velocity: a command to the right of the current position makes the eye move right. For the same mismatch, a larger response time $\tau$ gives a smaller velocity, so the eye takes longer to catch up and smooths rapid command changes more strongly. This response time belongs to the simplified eye filter; it is not automatically the synaptic decay time used in the memory model.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p120 sha256 03177a907301b9e678f307fdd80b75bf6bb748779998697b242e77837f2c9dad -->
p120, lines 120-120; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p118; next: p126

> $W$ is a Brownian process with unit increment variance per unit time, so $D$ has units deg$^2$/s. Define velocity $v=\dot E=(B-E)/\tau$. Taking its increment gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p126 sha256 e6a244842c79df64de991f24286c67e421ac4195c94efdc7ca78d717bf851983 -->
p126, lines 126-126; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p120; next: p128

> Velocity loses its past value over time $\tau$ and receives new random input. To add the surviving effect of those inputs, divide the past into short intervals of width $h$. The Brownian change $\Delta W_j=W(u_j+h)-W(u_j)$ has mean zero and variance $h$. Changes in disjoint intervals are independent. Its contribution to the velocity at time $t$ is approximately $(\sqrt{2D}/\tau)e^{-(t-u_j)/\tau}\Delta W_j$: the initial change has decayed between $u_j$ and $t$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p128 sha256 23202cc6ce511280959376246e260dd1042ca13aedf8bcfbbaee9462d725445a -->
p128, lines 128-128; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p126; next: p134

> The integral against $dW(u)$ denotes the limit of these weighted sums as $h$ tends to zero. It does not require an ordinary derivative of the Brownian path. After the initial velocity has decayed, extending the input history into the distant past gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p134 sha256 f9ba08be70d412bd4c74a1e31baad98348acedcc85eeeba07f94d0a5188cd4b2 -->
p134, lines 134-134; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p128; next: p146

> For a lag $s\geq0$, multiply the sums for $v(t)$ and $v(t+s)$ and average. Products of distinct increments have zero mean. The same increment contributes its variance $h$, so the sum becomes an ordinary overlap integral over their shared past:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p146 sha256 51f5037a9df187aa3bdb5eccebfa65ca6e3f38f840f2c0371457241042f68c1f -->
p146, lines 146-146; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p134; next: p157

> Here $r=t-u$ is the age of an input increment. Covariance is even in the lag, so $C_v(s)=(D/\tau)e^{-|s|/\tau}$ for either sign of $s$. Integrating velocity over an interval gives position change. The double integral over its covariance reduces to

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p157 sha256 9e017f94ea3a08ca3f895cb3f314a9531f130a3879b2a838c87dd868655fd426 -->
p157, lines 157-157; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p146; next: p163

> At short lags, $1-e^{-\ell/\tau}=\ell/\tau-\ell^2/(2\tau^2)+\cdots$, so

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p163 sha256 d88a03ac030220b4957eb65cbe06b4f6921ffa0944c5c18e7f1f0b8eff7081b6 -->
p163, lines 163-163; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p157; next: p169

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p169 sha256 0f0225fdb7b13b5c13ce5a86dc88f4a0bb0805e8dd2bef24d4e37507bd6bb53c -->
p169, lines 169-169; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p163; next: p171

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p171 sha256 1fad7d8ed9c8d7bcddd1a272a4f31db931d249414dc59687cffe2dfe2acfbb5d -->
p171, lines 171-171; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p169; next: p178

> Holding the internal diffusion coefficient $D$ fixed, increasing $\tau$ reduces short-lag displacement and moves the transition to linear growth to longer lags. It does not stop accumulating error: the long-lag MSD growth rate still approaches $2D$. Increasing $D$ instead raises the entire MSD curve in proportion, at fixed $\tau$. The local logarithmic slope of this MSD is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p178 sha256 baa5ad2c0d9c3aa7b8ef369d9d354b8e810dc2c67d1c0e0a20a243254e6d3ee6 -->
p178, lines 178-178; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p171; next: p180

> approaching two at short lags and one at long lags. At $\ell=\tau$ it is $e-1\simeq1.718$; at $\ell=5\tau$ it is about $1.239$. [Thus an intermediate slope can arise from filtering ordinary diffusion, without faster than Brownian accumulation inside the memory circuit.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p190 sha256 8cfbc13c0cff7b72f133d5496a69e191e70b4ff0a388913aad0f183dc708f688 -->

> The actual muscle response contains fast and slow exponential components, and the full model also contains motor neuron filtering and feedback. Those ingredients change the detailed curve. ## ch:EYE-CIRCUIT - A noisy memory circuit can produce eye drift

Source file: `content/chapters/04c-eye-circuit.md`

Purpose: A circuit that stores the intended eye angle is one possible source of fixational drift. This chapter follows the proposed model from interacting neurons through motor neurons and muscles to eye motion, and explains how delayed visual feedback can limit that motion.

Prerequisites: ch:EYE-DRIFT, ch:EYE-TIMING, ch:MEMORY-INFORMATION

Sidecar sections: A noisy integrator can reproduce drift after muscle filtering, Spike regularity and circuit parameters set the model's drift scale, The figures test shared drive, diffusion, and the full circuit model, The evidence places shared drive upstream without identifying one circuit

Blocks: 9 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

### A noisy integrator can reproduce drift after muscle filtering

Location: line 7, anchor `sec:EYE-CIRCUIT-MODEL`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 9
Closing passage line: 111

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p9 sha256 62f21c33212cae14df8f6751c9ee908cbe2c045d98ae43c25fc45a10a738dd1c -->
p9, lines 9-9; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p11

> The recording analysis places much of the drive upstream of OMNs. To test whether integrator noise could supply it, the authors build a model from a noisy memory circuit through to the eye. The model adapts an earlier goldfish integrator architecture to primate tuning and variability. The model has two groups of integrator neurons. One group fires more when the eye points farther right; the other fires more when it points farther left. Neurons excite cells in their own group and inhibit cells in the other group. The connection strengths are tuned so that the circuit can approximately maintain a range of represented eye positions.[^p2-network]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p11 sha256 2c55ee2c6b6164e165046b3628b1610bb123b45dafe2dc76be02dc1cdcf2a568 -->
p11, lines 11-11; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p9; next: p18

> For the right population, the model's rate is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p18 sha256 670a618c0d9e32cdfcced57c0ccebb39ddc8097dbe087aa1f4cefc79375840e2 -->
p18, lines 18-18; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p11; next: p20

> $E_{OI}$ is the internally represented position in degrees, $\zeta_i^R$ is a tuning slope in spikes/(s deg), and $\lambda_i^0$ is a rate offset in spikes/s. Taking the maximum with zero prevents negative firing. $F$ is a feedback input in degrees, defined below. The matching left neuron has the opposite slope, $\zeta_i^L=-\zeta_i^R$. In an active neuron, a change of one degree changes its rate by $\zeta_i$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p20 sha256 c7ff79e27f16380433894a1124e9b3b4e9a626f712e6e58ea8324738580c53cf -->
p20, lines 20-20; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p18; next: p28

> The model scales each spike train into an input $X_i$ with a saturating mean response. This input changes the neuron's synaptic output $S_i$, the lingering effect of its spikes on neurons receiving them. A weighted difference between the two groups' synaptic outputs gives the represented eye angle $E_{OI}$. This value sets the firing rates again, closing the feedback loop:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p28 sha256 af7902f51d056f623d1f9e3bfe92c3cd163f3085baba4c42d920c06b2e517fc8 -->
p28, lines 28-28; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p20; next: p30

> Here $\xi_i^R$ is a spike train generated at rate $\lambda_i^R$, $\tau_s$ is the synaptic decay time, and $\lambda_t=60$ Hz sets the rate scale in the nonlinear synaptic response. Treating spikes as counts, $X_i$ and $S_i$ are dimensionless, and the weights $\eta_i$ are in degrees. Between spikes, $S_i$ decays exponentially; one spike adds a jump of $1/[\tau_s(\lambda_t+\lambda_i^R)]$. Replacing spikes by their mean rate gives a drive $\lambda_i^R/(\lambda_t+\lambda_i^R)$ that approaches one as the rate increases. This is the model's saturating synaptic effect, rather than the purely linear synapses of the basic 2012 example.[^p2-network]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p30 sha256 f1993f1ed276e39b91f77540c6e110be2e516ea8ea611c4c6121c4592f38473d -->
p30, lines 30-30; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p28; next: p38

> Persistence requires a further condition on that feedback. Set $F=0$ and replace spikes by their mean rates. Define the mean saturated drives at a proposed stored value $z$ by

p38, lines 38-38; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p30; next: p46

p46, lines 46-46; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p38; next: p53

> [An exact continuum of persistent readout values would require $G(z)=z$ over the represented range.]{.key-statement} For a small displacement from a fixed value, Taylor expansion gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p53 sha256 88a9b4b7e2163a77f59b93460343c4cbf3f8bd3fd35b92844b56589551691747 -->
p53, lines 53-53; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p46; next: p55

> Thus $G'(z)=1$ is the local neutral condition for the stored coordinate. Stable directions of the remaining synaptic state are also needed for an attractor. The weights were optimized to approximate the fixed-point relation across $-50$ to $50$ degrees. [Residual mismatch can produce deterministic drift, so this finite optimized circuit provides approximate persistence rather than an exactly neutral continuum.]{.key-statement} The tuning slopes and thresholds were sampled to match reported macaque relationships and variation. With spikes, perturbations along the nearly neutral direction additionally accumulate. This reduction is a teaching derivation from the paper's equations; Figure 4 separates the resulting internal diffusion from later muscle smoothing.[^p2-network]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p55 sha256 16a7550faf237df16de1714225861cdde1377a31585f50fb80cadb6668e385df -->

> The motor neurons receive this position signal:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p72 sha256 dc789f13c057db91a7975ce8b20dae47a49a8bfc0a45404054f3ebe109469778 -->
p72, lines 72-72; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p64; next: p74

> $\lambda_i^M$ is a motor rate in spikes/s, $E_{T_i}$ is that neuron's position threshold in degrees, and $k_i$ is its position sensitivity. $\xi_i^M$ is its generated spike train. The filtered output $s_i^M$ also has units spikes/s, with decay time $\tau_{MN}$. One spike makes a jump of $1/\tau_{MN}$; at constant mean rate, the mean output is that rate.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p74 sha256 ddcb220d0dfb7202998c334244af8f386a828df6a8544acf98825fc373b6d8b8 -->
p74, lines 74-74; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p72; next: p81

> Each motor unit's muscle and plant response is represented by a position contribution $E_i$ satisfying

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p81 sha256 3c2d953faf07527bf3bcdf292f35e5db291ea8ddb0a0835a7b880a1c398e068f -->
p81, lines 81-81; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p74; next: p83

> These are Eqs. 28–31. The second order equation uses the same roles and units as the forward fit. At constant $E_{OI}$, an active unit has mean $s_i^M=k_i(E_{OI}-E_{T_i})$, giving mean $E_i=E_{OI}$ when derivatives vanish. Averaging therefore transmits the common position while reducing independent private fluctuations. This is an effective model of combined motor output, not a claim that one motor unit moves an isolated eye of its own.[^p2-motor]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p83 sha256 136a8be33cc7785e4a5baecf0bb1832cea6b8f9f8a858e32423042a32d9d832c -->
p83, lines 83-83; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p81; next: p85

> The model used diverse thresholds and response coefficients, with motor thresholds sampled between $-45$ and $-5$ degrees, and imposed $m_i<r_i^2/(4k_i)$ to give nonoscillatory individual response kernels. Main Methods report $N=30{,}000$ integrator neurons, $N_m=1{,}000$ OMNs, and $\tau_s=20$ ms for the main full simulation. The separate private OMN comparison in Fig. 3 used 2,000 OMNs. These neuron counts belong to different simulation comparisons.[^p2-motor]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p85 sha256 9d128461453cf9975df1673799977098c6bd812722c2fac5a80cd470ac0bffee -->
p85, lines 85-85; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p83; next: p92

> As the eye moves while viewing a stationary target, the target's image moves across the retina. The authors assume that visual feedback responds to this recent image motion rather than to an absolute target-position error. Their delayed feedback model is:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p92 sha256 f1c3e1b8536d884232bf191cae3b0f4b54993b477d5d5b3c76a30b37889dfb1c -->
p92, lines 92-92; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p85; next: p94

> Both positions are in degrees, so $A$ is dimensionless in this formulation. The main value is $A=0.015$. If gaze and its internal representation have recently moved rightward, the delayed position is to their left, making $F$ negative. Its contribution to the integrator rates then opposes continued rightward movement. For a slowly varying position that the eye tracks, $E(t-\tau_d)-E_{OI}(t)\simeq-\tau_d\dot E(t)$, showing the velocity-like action and sign. The approximation requires tracking and slow change over the delay.[^p2-feedback]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p94 sha256 da217aef1cd1d63f9de5c5e888d933b3fbebce6aeb8b8d9f9962888ac011a44d -->
p94, lines 94-94; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p92; next: p96

> The effect on the ongoing MSD must be checked from the coupled model, rather than inferred from the delay alone. A short MSD lag is a separation between two samples in a running process, not the time since a new perturbation. Past measurements can already affect both samples, and $F$ also contains the immediate term $-A E_{OI}(t)$. Extended Data Fig. 3 compares the model with and without feedback: feedback changes amplitude across lags, while its larger effect on logarithmic slope occurs at longer lags. Figure 5 shows the full model. [Muscle filtering supplies the short-lag smoothness, and feedback improves the detailed longer-lag fit. These are results of this model comparison, not a hard delay-based cutoff or a claim excluding visual control under other tasks.]{.key-statement}[^p2-feedback]

Essential explanation cannot be marked Not applicable. Trace the reader's task and its dependencies, including gaps not covered by other fields. Record unresolved: none only after resolving essential gaps; otherwise describe them and keep the unit verdict open. A complete checklist is not proof of complete teaching.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04c-eye-circuit.md::sec:EYE-SCALING -->

### Spike regularity and circuit parameters set the model's drift scale

Location: line 114, anchor `sec:EYE-SCALING`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 116
Closing passage line: 178

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p116 sha256 58baf4016022f58ff151e554fa607817a5c3eefc4937141aec9b3126b1d3fbba -->
p116, lines 116-116; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p111; next: p118

> An agreement in amplitude is informative only if we know how much freedom the model has. The paper uses regular spike trains. The [spike variability section](ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-poisson) defined the interspike interval coefficient of variation (CV) as the interval standard deviation divided by its mean. A Poisson process at constant rate has CV one; the model uses about 0.22 for integrator neurons and about 0.07 for OMNs. [Such firing is called [**sub-Poisson spiking**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} here because its long window count variability is lower than a Poisson process at the same rate, under the interval construction used in the model.]{.definition-explanation}[^p2-cv]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p118 sha256 426484c85a1fa9facac2aadc3757571dcf77e291c22e7490984488a5dad39d4f -->
p118, lines 118-118; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p116; next: p120

> The construction starts with a Poisson process at $M$ times the desired rate and retains every $M$th spike. At constant rate, a retained interval is the sum of $M$ independent exponential intervals. Each initial interval has mean $1/(M\lambda)$ and variance $1/(M\lambda)^2$. The retained interval therefore has mean $1/\lambda$, variance $1/(M\lambda^2)$, and CV $1/\sqrt M$. This preserves the mean rate while reducing variability. Its use at changing rates is part of the model; the simple interval derivation assumes a constant rate.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p120 sha256 2222fc486036d1a453a5d73e86b4b66143e0a0b5db8cd430596f3bdaabacb60f -->
p120, lines 120-120; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p118; next: p128

> At constant rate, the retained intervals are independent. [This is a [**renewal process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a spike process whose successive intervals are independent and identically distributed.]{.definition-explanation} Let their mean be $\mu=1/\lambda$ and standard deviation $\sigma_I$. The time to $n$ spikes has variance $n\sigma_I^2$. For a long fixed window $L\simeq n\mu$, a fluctuation in total interval time produces the opposite fluctuation in count divided by $\mu$. Thus

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p128 sha256 2bc134b802d4a537f31e94ff71be2e235dfbb232bd3bb07565c61e78c0d34fbf -->
p128, lines 128-128; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p120; next: p130

> This explains why the diffusion scale contains CV squared: it is the strength of the accumulated spike fluctuations relative to the Poisson case. The [diffusion calculation](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-diffusion) and [information bound](ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-bound) show how projecting those fluctuations onto a neutral memory direction adds a factor proportional to $1/\tau_s^2$ and, for a comparable independent population, $1/N$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p130 sha256 ea92a5944f6936bf14c08b8eb5a686309ccd3a64074dcb0fbd8b0fd3d02a5a2f -->
p130, lines 130-130; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p128; next: p136

> Rate and sensitivity need more care. Write the absolute local rate slope as $a=d\lambda/dE$, in spikes/(s deg). If $N$ similar cells fire near rate $\lambda_0$, their Poisson information per unit time about position is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p136 sha256 361a2c878568912eb901ca5bab4ef2f7d63e7d0202cf670dc8960c97eae9eaf1 -->
p136, lines 136-136; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p130; next: p143

> Using the [information-diffusion relation](ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-bound) as a scale, including the $\mathrm{CV}^2$ factor just derived and holding the network geometry fixed,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p143 sha256 a97e949c6ae8180c1ec415fd1e133720415886dedd04e3fdde70ef41afb02e93 -->
p143, lines 143-143; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p136; next: p149

> If instead sensitivity is expressed as the fractional slope $b=a/\lambda_0$, in inverse degrees, the same expression becomes

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p149 sha256 2108aa4383cb18b04d21fbe7e9e473ae6868faf76a9388fbc318d462f622b442 -->
p149, lines 149-151; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p143; next: p153

> This second form has the dependence printed in the paper's Eq. 1, $D\propto\mathrm{CV}^2N^{-1}\lambda_0^{-1}k_0^{-2}\tau_s^{-2}$. However, its surrounding text calls $k_0$ position sensitivity without specifying fractional normalization. If $k_0$ means an absolute slope in Hz/degree, that printed expression has the wrong units for $D$; the version with $\lambda_0$ in the numerator has the required units deg$^2$/s.  This scaling is a comparison under fixed network geometry and noise assumptions, not an exact formula for every recurrent circuit.[^p2-scaling]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p153 sha256 42108d5c0778252cbcd35d52f426aa9e76c329e923fd7b375471446ca7dd953a -->
p153, lines 153-153; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p149; next: p155

> At fixed network geometry and under the noise assumptions above, the scale changes as follows:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p155 sha256 34adbe757bd5affe2fb5b060f436bc1fca3237c2ff8374e071eb38fe8056c78b -->
p155, lines 155-155; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p153; next: p156

> Doubling $N$ halves it.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p156 sha256 97eca66015ea66b16c7b4fcd50e9b39053a11b090620a6593b38a378a7f8817d -->
p156, lines 156-156; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p155; next: p157

> Doubling CV multiplies it by four.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p157 sha256 7041a8942a6d60632e76aa235eebc13fc76689b7c5b5a105d7defaf4d65f0af1 -->
p157, lines 157-157; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p156; next: p158

> Doubling $\tau_s$ divides it by four.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p158 sha256 c69f6d406939a6610f247f8d90681c726715052db1426b91bbea5c0677918062 -->
p158, lines 158-158; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p157; next: p160

> Doubling the absolute slope at fixed rate also divides it by four.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p160 sha256 d580379a231f1928d507b8cc9ffee1e1a1ce2897c439f84943770e16eeced78e -->
p160, lines 160-160; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p158; next: p162

> [Increasing rate reduces diffusion when the fractional slope is fixed, because more spikes then also supply a proportionally steeper absolute slope. Increasing rate at fixed absolute slope is a different comparison and increases the scale in the first formula. A rate change alone does not specify which comparison is intended.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p162 sha256 96cd80b8d962767580705fe9c3c707439e072e0abeb2940437a471e7a6524b04 -->
p162, lines 162-162; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p160; next: p169

> Could the measured CV itself mostly reflect drift rather than private spike variability? Supplementary Notes test this. Let $\lambda(t)=\bar\lambda+aB(t)$ during a fixation of length $T$, with the rate changes small relative to $\bar\lambda$. For a Brownian path starting at zero, the expected time averaged squared position is $DT$, and the expected squared time mean is $2DT/3$. Subtracting gives the expected within-segment position variance $DT/3=2DT/6$:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p169 sha256 72378fdd398d6e247d1a7cc8ca3b5e733fe43ff9b996e8ca59dadc25f329176a -->
p169, lines 169-171; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p162; next: p178

> The second term follows from $\mathbb E[B(t)B(s)]=2D\min(t,s)$ and $T^{-2}\int_0^T\int_0^T2D\min(t,s)\,dt\,ds=2DT/3$. Expanding $1/(\bar\lambda+aB)$ to first order gives $1/\bar\lambda-aB/\bar\lambda^2$. Dividing its standard deviation by its mean then gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p178 sha256 e5b6617e73dc132c58fd249a77b9dba9c1d4461bbaac83be5095e135cc171db0 -->
p178, lines 178-178; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p169; next: p182

> With the supplement's values $a=1/15$ spikes/(s arcmin), $2D=100$ arcmin$^2$/s, $T=0.5$ s, and $\bar\lambda=80$ Hz, this is about $0.0024$, well below the OMN CV near 0.07. These are an order of magnitude check, not a fitted diffusion constant for the whole model. Since one degree is 60 arcmin, that $2D$ equals $0.0278$ deg$^2$/s, or $D=0.0139$ deg$^2$/s.[^p2-cv-drift]

p190, lines 190-190; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p188; next: p192

> The most useful supplemental comparisons are Extended Data Fig. 1 for individual uncertainty, Fig. 2 for noise subtraction and its simulated control, Fig. 3 for feedback, Fig. 4 for a no-central-drive control, and Fig. 5 for the other monkey's fit. In the no-central-drive control, $\widehat\chi=-0.09\pm0.22$ is compatible with zero; that negative estimate is sampling variation. Figures 6–8 document cell classification, segment sampling, and a rank correlation check. Figure 9 shows the heterogeneous model parameters. Figure 10 separates spiking noise passed directly to the output from spiking noise that drives diffusion inside the integrator: internal stochastic spikes remain in the latter comparison. Its caption reports little direct output-noise effect beyond 50 ms.[^p2-supp-figures]

p248, lines 248-248; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p243; next: p250

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p250 sha256 13eb9938bf9c6236208fc52e01c9ec1269b4cf02460ab63085476a39b268d851 -->
p250, lines 250-250; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p248; next: p251

> The common variance estimate supports a drive upstream of OMNs, given the calibration and noise assumptions.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p251 sha256 0e5d9875c42e7d1eba8d510a5e8714c4a462cd405dbbf43cc17a69bdb9d4f8c6 -->
p251, lines 251-251; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p250; next: p252

> The MSD comparison supports a command that accumulates variation over the measured interval, rather than stationary white noise passed through a decaying muscle filter.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p252 sha256 a53b626e8087382134210e2ebd356cd81cc400d4dac48406313cec70350ad76b -->
p252, lines 252-252; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p251; next: p254

> The circuit model shows that integrator noise with plausible parameters can generate such a command and explain its filtered amplitude and timing.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p254 sha256 d06b6c83910a80bd1ee557c221451c776f0c6704836cfa7d844d1016289a0a60 -->
p254, lines 254-254; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p252; next: p256

> Those results do not uniquely locate the noise inside integrator neurons. Two missing tests limit that conclusion:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p256 sha256 60d3b8c4b2e125b9ad975602b8cee5a697c525c785976bd88b6e38fae52c497b -->
p256, lines 256-256; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p254; next: p257

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p257 sha256 617ad29267837c3232f4196d91fd6ba06c2ea3c2d71dbb9ea776f3ddffac9cc6 -->
p257, lines 257-257; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p256; next: p259

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p259 sha256 ba65fa56bc0d3faafbfc995dc46aba3604de7dcd48e6d201336e7ff0644e2fd9 -->
p259, lines 259-259; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p257; next: p261

> [Noisy inputs to the integrator, or an already diffusive upstream signal, remain possible. The authors make this distinction in the Discussion and explicitly in their peer review response. Several combinations of neuron count, variability, and synaptic time constant can also fit an amplitude, so matching one curve does not uniquely recover those parameters.]{.key-statement}[^p2-localization]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p261 sha256 e90133b47eae4fd2b41bea14ed4fbcd84fab6245e8e39ca2a6b45d74f07524b8 -->
p261, lines 261-261; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p259; next: none

> The study's empirical scope is horizontal motion during these fixation segments in two animals. Visual tasks, feedback, and other sources of input can alter the statistics. Its contribution to the connection with the 2012 theory is more precise: a continuous memory can diffuse internally, while a mechanical output of that memory looks smooth and superdiffusive at shorter lags. To compare a memory theory with behavior, the mapping from stored value to measured movement is part of the calculation.

## ch:PLACE-FIELDS - Field counts and gaps reveal threshold and scale

Source file: `content/chapters/05-place-fields.md`

Purpose: Hippocampal place cells fire more at particular locations as an animal moves. This chapter introduces the rodent and bat recordings, then shows how a smooth random input that exceeds a firing threshold can produce several separate active regions for one cell.

Prerequisites: ch:FOUNDATIONS, ch:PERSISTENCE

Sidecar sections: Thresholding a smooth Gaussian input produces place fields, Upcrossings count fields, with an endpoint correction, Mean field and gap lengths separate threshold from scale

Blocks: 3 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05-place-fields.md::ch:PLACE-FIELDS -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p3 sha256 49741c56d24c86291963aa1db0187fb8ff7235c96482abe531bd35fd47c246e1 -->
p3, lines 3-3; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p5

> The study compares recordings of hippocampal place cells from bats flying through tunnels, rats moving over an arena floor, and bats flying through a room. For tunnel flights, the analysis uses distance along an approximately one-dimensional route, although the tunnel itself has width and height. The floor map uses two position coordinates, and the room map uses three.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p5 sha256 6690f9b94e6e8ae6d89fe5007cc730d3276602809cbb5c4f1b71272581befb9f -->
p5, lines 5-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p7

> A cell recorded on a short track may have one smooth firing hill. [On a long track, the same kind of cell may have several separated fields, each with a different width and height. In a room, its active regions can bend, split, and surround inactive holes. How could one model produce this variety and still make useful predictions?]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 de541371446a001ae1c4837cc0e011d3d9e4ce2bd71330666d4e68dba6c6cf51 -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p9

> Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak propose that a cell's spatial input is a smooth random function. This function can be interpreted as the average combined input arriving through the cell's synapses at each animal position. The model cell has positive firing rate where this input exceeds a threshold. The recordings estimate firing rates rather than measuring this input directly.[^p3-model] [The model predicts distributions of field lengths, areas, and volumes, rather than the location of a particular cell's next field. Its strongest tests ask whether fitting field sizes and counts also explains field shapes and holes.]{.key-statement}[^p3-question]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p9 sha256 e1e6980f6d60db858b1d25b5719fef33a26036773dc5a7b975a816b801d9852b -->
p9, lines 9-9; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p7; next: p11

> We begin on a track, where a field starts at [an [**upcrossing**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a point where the input crosses upward through the threshold.]{.definition-explanation} Counting these crossings gives the average number of fields. Combining that count with the lengths of active fields and inactive gaps then separates the effect of the threshold from the distance over which the map changes. These calculations establish the parameters needed for the later predictions of sizes and shapes.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p11 sha256 8fdab90b4b34de6de1bcb6d39e9aae308c919ff7378e7b6d6cf2b4cbe993a21c -->
p11, lines 11-11; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p9; next: p15

> [Spatial randomness here describes different possible rate maps. Once a map is drawn, it is fixed while the animal moves through it. The spatial coordinate is not time, and the model does not by itself describe memory drift, spike timing, or learning.]{.key-statement}
