### Thresholding a smooth Gaussian input produces place fields

Location: line 13, anchor `sec:PLACE-GP`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 15
Closing passage line: 93

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p15 sha256 cf3931630bd66d71de8518d443fc4b71f41ade0a3e68c41d522d4c3108417994 -->
p15, lines 15-15; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p11; next: p17

> To predict a field's width, we need to relate activity at nearby positions. A histogram of firing rates alone loses that information: rearranging its values could turn one broad field into many tiny fields without changing the histogram.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p17 sha256 86a390da6d0d6dc37ba234e189086e68aa3b3d8e8cb12c0c1e054c665950caef -->
p17, lines 17-17; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p15; next: p21

> The model assigns an input $h(x)$ to each position $x$, with nearby values linked so that a cell can have a continuous active region. [A **Gaussian process** specifies a distribution of such maps.]{.definition-explanation} Its mean sets the average input, and its spatial covariance describes how inputs at two positions vary together across possible maps. The chosen covariance must also make the maps smooth enough for the derivatives used below. After a map is drawn, moving the animal changes which position is evaluated, not the map itself.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p21 sha256 fa629660ffd87f8cb5eb6e12cf71105ec229d063ea49f16ff014b7eaae42c538 -->
p21, lines 21-21; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p17; next: p28

> [A [**Gaussian process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is a probability distribution over functions such that the values at any finite set of positions form a jointly Gaussian random vector.]{.definition-explanation} For positions $x_1,\ldots,x_n$, the vector $(h(x_1),\ldots,h(x_n))$ therefore has a multivariate Gaussian distribution. Its mean vector and covariance matrix are obtained by evaluating two functions,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p28 sha256 5e4b70bab10a788cc895f1619e1de1d2293aa56d77107c632a8ca1b924417586 -->
p28, lines 28-28; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p21; next: p32

> The expectation runs over possible maps, or over cells assumed to share these statistics. These two functions determine every finite joint distribution. A Gaussian process can thus produce many different curves while keeping a precise rule for their spatial dependence. [A [**jointly Gaussian vector**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is one for which every linear combination of its entries is Gaussian.]{.definition-explanation} For this family, zero covariance between two subvectors implies their independence. That extra property, which does not hold for general distributions, will simplify the crossing calculation. The values after thresholding will generally not have a Gaussian distribution.[^p3-model]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p32 sha256 d7eef26ec39623ebdec3dffdc340f6e1f1b0ead389f12766cc2cbb9e896c627d -->
p32, lines 32-32; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p28; next: p34

> Three assumptions have different jobs:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p34 sha256 ecc954d992125f4b4841209594a99da5d9b2c1368783d4be98ae71824c273c20 -->
p34, lines 34-34; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p32; next: p35

> [[**Stationarity**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means that moving the observation window does not change the map's probability law. Here it gives a constant mean and a covariance $C(x,y)=C(y-x)$ that depends on separation.]{.definition-explanation} It does not say that a particular map is flat.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p35 sha256 9790274d1531bc4d767989f6fa7b654ea5fa3b611b5dfd7fc91fb4e60f3935af -->
p35, lines 35-35; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p34; next: p36

> [[**Isotropy**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means that rotating the separation does not change the covariance: in several dimensions, $C(\Delta x)$ depends only on $\|\Delta x\|$.]{.definition-explanation} It rules out a preferred direction in the model's statistics. Stationarity alone does not imply isotropy.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p36 sha256 918c7f153e6c52c9c3c244626b4ce88ba16b6ffceccb4d0deaa7b7684cc627a5 -->
p36, lines 36-36; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p35; next: p38

> [[**Smoothness**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} controls whether the map has well defined spatial derivatives.]{.definition-explanation} Crossing calculations require differentiable sample functions and nondegenerate joint distributions: the relevant vector of heights and derivatives must have an invertible covariance matrix, rather than obey an exact linear constraint. Peak calculations require second derivatives and enough regularity to control their local approximation.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p38 sha256 5a6c710f5eb1fa49372466cb9cee2871bd9af0811a97934e8b588612e815ad85 -->
p38, lines 38-38; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p36; next: p40

> A Gaussian distribution of values does not guarantee this smoothness. For example, a covariance proportional to $e^{-|x-y|/b}$ has a sharp corner at zero separation and does not supply the differentiable paths assumed below.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p40 sha256 f19aba453f15007b3477fd225c1ceac7a62a4b58c1fa0af170d4819308371576 -->
p40, lines 40-40; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p38; next: p48

> Set the mean to zero and write $\sigma_h^2=C(0)$ for input variance. A constant input mean can be absorbed into the threshold. It is useful to normalize the input,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p48 sha256 ce7eb0a4f37f7c549990ceb25320beb5eaa6985bcc1dda9d3463fb491977207b -->
p48, lines 48-48; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p40; next: p50

> Here $z$ has variance one, $q$ is a dimensionless threshold, and $a>0$ converts normalized input to firing rate, in Hz. The paper writes $\theta$ for $q$, $r$ for covariance, and $D$ for spatial dimension; this guide uses $q,C,d$ to avoid conflicts with earlier chapters. The original input threshold is $q\sigma_h$. In the paper's Equation 3 the firing rate is proportional to $[h-q\sigma_h]_+$, which is equivalent to this form after choosing the gain.[^p3-model]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p50 sha256 a6b1ad4cb5499653ecbf7b47a460d1e529731cb76c04082ecb9ec1fec16005ef -->
p50, lines 50-50; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p48; next: p52

> The input threshold summarizes how much combined input is needed before the model cell fires. It can reflect the cell's own response properties and inhibitory input. Raising $q$ at a fixed input map leaves fewer positions active. If the absolute threshold above the input mean is positive and held fixed, increasing the input standard deviation $\sigma_h$ lowers $q$, making activity more widespread. Changing $a$ instead changes rates within active regions while preserving their zero-rate boundaries. A fixed positive detection cutoff in Hz would change this last conclusion. [A fitted threshold describes the balance of input and response; it does not identify which biological quantity changed.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p52 sha256 86a4397dc4e6ababff47289f83f408a10560f133d0d3e5143cceae036c94dbd8 -->
p52, lines 52-54; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p50; next: p58

> <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure"> <summary>Original paper figure: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025), Figure 1</summary> <div class="publisher-fold-body">

p58, lines 58-59; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p52; next: p61

> [The [**excursion set**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} above $q$ is]{.definition-explanation}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p67 sha256 bd77da23fb70c16405baa0ae9370ceee806bc92fd8604f134744a1b0d4c67a7f -->
p67, lines 67-67; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p61; next: p69

> Each connected piece of $A_q$ is a model place field. In one dimension its size is length. In two and three dimensions it is area and volume. [Two nearby peaks belong to one field if an above-threshold path joins them. Counting peaks and counting fields therefore answer different questions.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p69 sha256 13a7443113bd7235868647853097382186a2f42f844defb231ad1430a9d7d59a -->
p69, lines 69-69; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p67; next: p76

> For a one dimensional stationary process, differentiation of covariance gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p76 sha256 2442f626a333929c7774a980d8033033c9da5bffa9887ab195db0428a595466a -->
p76, lines 76-76; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p69; next: p83

> The minus sign comes from differentiating $y-x$ once with respect to each variable. The paper defines the spatial scale

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p83 sha256 766c616e0154b3bb719c9924855db5e4e8fa62cfd08baac4f9c34f39bbdc3798 -->
p83, lines 83-83; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p76; next: p85

> Thus $\sigma$ compares typical height with typical slope and has units of distance. In an isotropic field, the same definition applies along any unit direction. For the smooth Gaussian covariance $C(\Delta x)=\sigma_h^2\exp[-\|\Delta x\|^2/(2\sigma^2)]$, it is exactly the parameter in that exponent. The paper uses a Gaussian covariance for its simulations. Other smooth covariances can share this local scale while having different longer distance structure.[^p3-scale]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p85 sha256 ebdd8b0016bad74b9e16ae42e4d433c8876d5071b8d33a62976ee07fd385b961 -->
p85, lines 85-85; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p83; next: p87

> A simulated Gaussian input map with covariance $C(\Delta x)=\exp[-(\Delta x/\sigma)^2/2]$ gives the firing map below when $q=0.8$ and $a=6$ Hz. Position is shown in units of $\sigma$.

Both panels describe the same fixed map over space, so neither curve is a trajectory over time. See [Gaussian input and firing rate, STAR Methods Eqs. 2–3, p. e1](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=13).

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05-place-fields.md::sec:PLACE-KAC-RICE -->

### Upcrossings count fields, with an endpoint correction

Location: line 95, anchor `sec:PLACE-KAC-RICE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 97
Closing passage line: 153

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p97 sha256 26ffca6fa7d665145bde5d732371eea7a7bdad1b317e9e61fe6026147c797e0a -->
p97, lines 97-97; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p93; next: p99

> A field starts at an upcrossing, so its expected count can be found by counting upward passages through the threshold. A small interval around each crossing has a width determined by its slope; this lets us count crossings without locating every root.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p99 sha256 89750673492344d837763a92c89b3cd3f64839f3a984d2cf64b05477dd7eb0ef -->
p99, lines 99-99; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p97; next: p105

> Consider a differentiable fixed curve and a simple upward crossing $x_0$ of $q$, with slope $v=z'(x_0)>0$. Close to the crossing,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p105 sha256 88300f83b3042d9d68ffb844404be3d5628ff455cda5537ca320f3595d09e90f -->
p105, lines 105-105; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p99; next: p111

> The curve lies in the vertical band $|z-q|<\varepsilon$ for a spatial distance approximately $2\varepsilon/v$. Integrating $z'(x)/(2\varepsilon)$ over that interval contributes one. Restricting to positive slopes counts only upward crossings. [Under the usual regularity conditions, averaging this counting identity gives the [**Kac–Rice formula**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} for the mean upcrossing density,]{.definition-explanation}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p111 sha256 b2ca6c87c97a8ddc19b2da7ea251d7f81efcce17ed2d440c38075a5b5330f8ca -->
p111, lines 111-111; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p105; next: p113

> The density $p_{z,z'}(q,v)$ describes how often height is near $q$ and slope near $v$. Multiplication by $v$ compensates for the shorter spatial window of a steep crossing. Multiplying the probability of being above threshold by a typical slope would miss this weighting.[^p3-crossings]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p113 sha256 30afaa7d71d1c5c40139ed8744ebc9512d14702cffbd326bfdd18012244a2666 -->
p113, lines 113-113; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p111; next: p115

> A fixed smooth curve shows how to count a field already active at the left edge and why the crossing integral weights each slope.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p115 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p115, lines 115-115; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p113; next: p119

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p119 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p119, lines 119-119; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p115; next: p121

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p121 sha256 04bdc367a22c8716b8aab8fa75de3b51d243dc6ec03720e6789b1433a55ff7e3 -->
p121, lines 121-121; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p119; next: p123

> The count includes every connected active interval intersecting the track. Discarding clipped boundary fields would change this convention.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p123 sha256 8c29e2b994c75073980c3f8ad32714b819c26ccc7b35bd53d2de20a9b6433dc4 -->
p123, lines 123-123; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p121; next: p129

> We can evaluate the integral because height and slope are independent here. Stationarity gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p129 sha256 84fa962b3b57a4ad77ef7daa267fe687fa4c1e5f87b1c643090fb3b1ab3d7c5a -->
p129, lines 129-129; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p123; next: p143

> Height and slope are jointly Gaussian; zero covariance therefore makes them independent. Their distributions are $z\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$ and $z'\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^{-2})$. Consequently,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p143 sha256 63ae735d8aaef1bef578603bbf14c00cbc97f982ff0b0a5ed6547eb6f68824be -->
p143, lines 143-143; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p129; next: p145

> In the integral, use $u=\sigma^2v^2/2$, so $v\,dv=du/\sigma^2$. [This result is exact for the stated smooth stationary Gaussian model. A high threshold is not needed.]{.key-statement} Its units are inverse distance: increasing $\sigma$ stretches the map and reduces crossings per metre.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p145 sha256 a3178d05daf5a8383331b6a0a5b1c5e79a3b9e27e140138effb0735de2e92ce6 -->
p145, lines 145-145; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p143; next: p153

> On a finite track $[0,L]$, each upcrossing starts a field, but a field may already be active at the left endpoint. If $N_L$ counts all components intersecting the track, then, under regularity conditions that exclude tangencies and give nonzero slopes at crossings,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p153 sha256 7736661f9fb1708487ba89d75f45e334b953e0f0ba02c2c2a3f68d5fa0f24dad -->
p153, lines 153-153; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p145; next: p157

> where $Q(q)=1-\Phi(q)$ and $\Phi$ is the standard normal cumulative distribution function. The last term is the endpoint correction. Removing incomplete boundary fields changes the counting convention, so the same correction cannot then be used unchanged. This identity also gives the paper's exact one dimensional mean Euler characteristic, Equation 8.[^p3-ec-source]

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05-place-fields.md::sec:PLACE-INFERENCE -->

### Mean field and gap lengths separate threshold from scale

Location: line 155, anchor `sec:PLACE-INFERENCE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 157
Closing passage line: 200

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p157 sha256 6c14fa70c37f2886286fdd9b503c382a38ef32e4e25437d77db2fa616279e50d -->
p157, lines 157-157; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p153; next: p159

> [A low field count can result from a high threshold or a slowly varying map. Field lengths help separate those possibilities.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p159 sha256 3ba9275df1f7ac4137a1a3e303c3f773156791a58bfb30831959120f36dbfd87 -->
p159, lines 159-159; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p157; next: p170

> At a uniformly chosen position, the probability of activity is $Q(q)$. Hence a track of length $L$ has expected occupied length $LQ(q)$. On a long stationary track, the average occupied length per field is the occupied length per unit distance divided by the number of fields per unit distance. The corresponding gap calculation uses the inactive fraction $\Phi(q)$:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p170 sha256 6fc750fb0ae48d8804aa2e0a57ba3d7dfaf42a38e0f59cb9564ec1a8f7ab25fd -->
p170, lines 170-170; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p159; next: p172

> These are stationary per-field and per-gap means, ignoring clipping at track boundaries. They are not an assertion that the expectation of a finite sample's ratio equals the ratio of its expectations. Subject to suitable averaging over a long track or many maps, they give the inference rule in Methods S3, Equations S14–S15.[^p3-means]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p172 sha256 302cfe3ed35e922f0fd2c191f88efb35fc98f0654a85332d71b8cd317ac46e33 -->
p172, lines 172-172; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p170; next: p179

> Adding and dividing removes one unknown at a time:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p179 sha256 735730f52e5c6f810adc36cacc71e4c2fa20715b18de9638770a73cd88db0e2e -->
p179, lines 179-179; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p172; next: p187

> Thus

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p187 sha256 a4f2ea777c7db9fea0029a5b14dc58a73452ea71201e76fc031fbcc2c66d9ab7 -->
p187, lines 187-187; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p179; next: p189

> For example, $q=1.1$ and $\sigma=2.4$ m, the fitted parameters reported for the 200 m bat tunnel, give $Q(q)=0.1357$, $\nu_+=0.03621$ per metre, $\mu_s=3.746$ m, and $\mu_g=23.868$ m in the continuous model. Putting these two means into the inversion recovers $q=1.1$ and $\sigma=2.4$ m. The expected number of intersecting fields on a 200 m track is $7.2425+0.1357=7.3782$, including the left endpoint term. These are calculations from the fitted model, not fresh estimates from raw recordings.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p189 sha256 95167b65831b39d98b556f3d53df59be93b7f0816e386e4ea1313a8c7b911dfb -->
p189, lines 189-189; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p187; next: p191

> [The means do not imply a full gap or count distribution. At high thresholds, distant rare excursions can approach independent events if correlations decay sufficiently with distance. In that regime, field locations approach a Poisson process, counts in a long interval are approximately Poisson, and gaps are approximately exponential. Smoothness and stationarity alone do not ensure this independence.]{.key-statement} A periodic Gaussian process, for example, retains long distance dependence. Upward and downward crossings that enclose the same small field remain strongly related even when fields far apart are almost independent.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p191 sha256 d8329140074a8cfa78d595d1fb497bf52c7bb51a0b4851af51cb62d749a92fe0 -->
p191, lines 191-191; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p189; next: p200

> Experimental selection also matters. If cells with no fields are excluded, a Poisson model with unconditioned mean $\lambda$ gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p200 sha256 3889a03387b2f0ea6253d52ac9eddfcfc693e052e1672ed237823cf0423ca7d5 -->
p200, lines 200-200; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p191; next: none

> The mean follows because the omitted $n=0$ term contributes zero to $\sum n\Pr(N=n)=\lambda$, while conditioning divides all remaining probabilities by $1-e^{-\lambda}$. For $\lambda=1$, $36.8\%$ of cells are excluded and the observed mean is $1.582$, not one. Using $1.582$ as the unconditioned mean would infer too many fields. The paper applies this correction to its two dimensional count fit, Equation 5.[^p3-selection]


Claim-bearing content: none

Claims to check:

- Claim c1, line 161: "$$ \begin{aligned} \mu_s&=\frac{Q(q)}{\nu_+(q)} =2\pi\sigma Q(q)e^{q^2/2},\\ \mu_g&=\frac{\Phi(q)}{\nu_+(q)} =2\pi\sigma\Phi(q)e^{q^2/2}. \end{aligned} $$" -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c2, line 170: "Subject to suitable averaging over a long track or many maps, they give the inference rule in Methods S3, Equations S14–S15.[^p3-means]" -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]

## ch:PLACE-SIZES - High peaks explain size laws across dimensions

Source file: `content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md`

Purpose: A place cell’s active regions can have different sizes on a route, across a floor, or in a flight volume. This chapter derives why the spatial model predicts different field-size distributions in one, two, and three dimensions, and identifies the high-threshold approximation used in that derivation.

Prerequisites: ch:PLACE-FIELDS

Sidecar sections: Near a high peak, the input is nearly parabolic, Geometry gives different size laws in each dimension, Slices test spatial structure beyond field volume, Why slopes at field boundaries are Rayleigh

Blocks: 6 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md::ch:PLACE-SIZES -->

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p3 sha256 c1c5ed61ce08fdc26629abc2e27dbe649cba8f24890823bb09759f80876f60f3 -->
p3, lines 3-3; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p5

> [Knowing the average field length does not tell us how often very small or very large fields occur. Why should the same spatial model give one size distribution on a track, another in an arena, and a third in a room?]{.key-statement} The next step is to find the shape of a typical field when only unusually high input peaks rise above the threshold.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p5 sha256 7ecb9e61407341e67ccc361de30c982419a33444cad19d0f41c3da25025d6b6b -->
p5, lines 5-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p7

> Near such a peak, the smooth input is approximately a downward parabola. Its height above the threshold sets a radius, and converting that radius into length, area or volume gives the different size laws. Cuts through a map test its spatial structure, and slopes at field edges provide a separate check on the predicted field widths. [The size laws use a high threshold approximation; the boundary-slope law has a separate exact derivation under the smooth stationary Gaussian assumptions.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 26a1fe7914be17a1e3c5a4d6f987ea769b07b29a63e572b3b3b67ef972df4555 -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p11

> We keep the earlier notation: $z(x)$ is the spatial input normalized to variance one, $q$ is its threshold, $\sigma$ is its local distance scale, and $a$ converts positive excess input into firing rate. The number of spatial dimensions is $d$. The firing rule remains $F(x)=a[z(x)-q]_+$, where $[u]_+=\max(u,0)$.

> Here $\delta_{ij}$ is one for equal indices and zero otherwise. For jointly Gaussian variables, conditioning $Y$ on a scalar $Z$ of variance one gives $\mathbb E[Y\mid Z=b]=\operatorname{Cov}(Y,Z)b$ when both means vanish. One way to see this is to write $Y=\operatorname{Cov}(Y,Z)Z+\epsilon$; the remainder has zero covariance with $Z$ and is therefore independent of it. Applying this to each Hessian entry gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p35 sha256 47d4b1cf84b128232a6a68f53843a3ec5ff5371866328fc9cd3b8a9d22498674 -->

> The conditional fluctuations around this mean have a variance that does not grow with $b$. For a high peak, the negative mean curvature is of order $q$, so its relative fluctuations become small. Conditioning the gradient to zero does not change this Gaussian conditional mean, because the gradient has zero covariance with both height and Hessian in a stationary isotropic field. Selecting an actual maximum also weights curvatures, but its leading high peak shape is the same.

Figure 3D reports $1.75$ versus $1.71$ using cube root of volume in 3D. These are compatible with a model that approaches exponent two only as the threshold becomes large; they are not measurements of the limiting exponent. Figure 5A–C illustrates the progression toward simpler, usually single peak fields as $q$ increases. Figure 4C tests this trend in the shorter bat tunnel.[^p3-shapes]

Write the strongest correction a domain expert would make to this unit's text or figures; set kind to correctness or wording; name its target passage or figure label; set disposition to fixed or does not apply; give the reason. An objection of none is not a review. The strongest correction is the one that would change what a reader believes or does. Aim first at what the unit claims: a promise word such as always, once, ensures or remains that is false or needs a condition; a named method described wrongly; code that does not do what the text says; a table cell; a diagnostic sequence that skips a stage; a verb without an actor; a label naming something other than what is drawn; a figure that disagrees with the text or another figure; and example content found in neither the chapter nor its sources. For each code listing, write test: followed by the path, relative to the guide, of a test that runs it on the inputs the text makes claims about, or not run: followed by the reason and how its claims were checked instead.
Essential explanation cannot be marked Not applicable. Trace the reader's task and its dependencies, including gaps not covered by other fields. Record unresolved: none only after resolving essential gaps; otherwise describe them and keep the unit verdict open. A complete checklist is not proof of complete teaching.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md::sec:PLACE-SIZE-LAW -->

### Geometry gives different size laws in each dimension

Location: line 91, anchor `sec:PLACE-SIZE-LAW`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 93
Closing passage line: 178

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p93 sha256 1901fc4e63d3a5b50382c5e7752d17da5da6af0c255516e359814c81b92aeae6 -->
p93, lines 93-93; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p88; next: p95

> A round region's radius and its volume have different units and different distributions. This geometric conversion explains why the model predicts different histograms in a track, an arena, and a room.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p95 sha256 9c8a12ab71aff30a3dc04fceee8cd369033919ef749dd4e1871a394025152900 -->
p95, lines 95-95; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p93; next: p97

> Let $v_d=\pi^{d/2}/\Gamma(1+d/2)$ be the volume of a unit ball in $d$ dimensions. For $d=1,2,3$, it is $2,\pi,4\pi/3$. [The symbol $\Gamma$ is the [**gamma function**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, defined for $t>0$ by $\Gamma(t)=\int_0^\infty u^{t-1}e^{-u}\,du$. It extends factorials: $\Gamma(n+1)=n!$.]{.definition-explanation}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p97 sha256 2622397a97e4137834c9aecff9dee82065045328ad11e47745542a699a321117 -->
p97, lines 97-97; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p95; next: p106

> Using the radius from the preceding section, the field size is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p106 sha256 a27858dfe6bc00b5646aaedf4ca12865baf30d61a2c1db5ab3646c520bc7b61d -->
p106, lines 106-106; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p97; next: p108

> The same local radius gives a length, an area, or a volume depending on the dimension of the region.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p108 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p108, lines 108-108; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p106; next: p112

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p112 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p112, lines 112-112; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p108; next: p114

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p114 sha256 c97d61d1ac8d110a78bcba1edfb10f00d1d6aa7b15ff1b2cae87917883a91774 -->
p114, lines 114-114; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p112; next: p116

> The round shape and exponential excess belong to the high-threshold limit, so they do not make every measured place field spherical.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p116 sha256 d7015fd40a4d70397a639649766ed3dab80232ac55cec5c395bb28e2c7e89e0c -->
p116, lines 116-116; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p114; next: p125

> Since the density of $U$ is $e^{-u}$ for $u>0$, changing variables gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p125 sha256 a21700950f13cea61955cf68feb5958b2567b3e5be5df2609be8d48ac0ec2752 -->
p125, lines 125-125; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p116; next: p127

> This is the paper's Equation 1. The expression for $\beta$ above is its leading high threshold geometric scale, not an exact finite threshold identity. A finite threshold simulation can share the approximate shape while having a different fitted scale.[^p3-size-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p127 sha256 70e635652302213a2cf731e3084b884490fbb2f91b7de49476aeffc988347e81 -->
p127, lines 127-127; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p125; next: p136

> The mean and its corresponding scale are

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p136 sha256 bd78903b899f2ba416d0a9fa0e50e6848ecdffcdf5af8bfb1f67d84cbd4075bb -->
p136, lines 136-136; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p127; next: p146

> Normalization needs no special integral: $\int_0^\infty p_d(s)\,ds=\int_0^\infty e^{-u}\,du=1$. The same substitution supplies every positive moment,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p146 sha256 e0cefd2bef21d8c57c06ba03819d1f74f6d0f41e3ad03b4ed921afc4a741b404 -->
p146, lines 146-146; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p136; next: p148

> Size has units of distance to the power $d$, while $\beta$ has units of inverse distance squared in every dimension. This makes $\beta s^{2/d}$ dimensionless, and makes $p_d(s)$ a density per unit size. These checks catch mistakes in changing variables.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p148 sha256 5eb4bde29d56abc33274fa983b0769051b44b928b70a23d40313217e78de35aa -->
p148, lines 148-148; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p146; next: p150

> Writing dimensionless size as $w=\beta^{d/2}s$ removes the size scale. The dimensional size laws then differ in shape, while the transformed variable $U=\beta s^{2/d}=w^{2/d}$ has the same exponential law.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p150 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p150, lines 150-150; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p148; next: p154

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p154 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p154, lines 154-154; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p150; next: p156

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p156 sha256 f3deb54ce8a9e5943cb5082baae607c47048ff2203832e1ac17c5f91a6a0cb9f -->
p156, lines 156-156; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p154; next: p158

> Removing units does not erase the difference between length, area, and volume distributions. The nonlinear transformation to U removes that shape difference; its shared exponential law is the assumption used in the change of variables. See [Field-size laws, Eq. 1, p. 1111](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=3).

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p158 sha256 9960784fd6b96a12fbf5b8855619bf1a26dae079bce219c3b96e0c64ec075ad2 -->
p158, lines 158-158; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p156; next: p162

> <div class="table-responsive">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p162 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p162, lines 162-162; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p158; next: p164

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p164 sha256 77645c45999217b7e2d49bdc6f90f817af8e5be7bbc6027c15f3cb896a1e1fd6 -->
p164, lines 164-164; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p162; next: p166

> [A [**Weibull distribution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} has survival probability $\exp[-(s/b)^k]$ for size $s>0$, scale $b>0$, and shape $k>0$.]{.definition-explanation} Here $k=2/d$ and $b=\beta^{-d/2}$. In 3D the diverging density does not mean a positive probability of exactly zero volume; the probability of $0<s<\delta$ still tends to zero as $\delta\to0$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p166 sha256 1198b5a52fcf5236f4556770b0651c726dee0edcbce5726be8f473d67ea222db -->
p166, lines 166-166; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p164; next: p168

> For a concrete comparison, set $\sigma=1$ m and $q=5$ in this limiting calculation. The mean size is $0.5013$ m in 1D, $0.2513$ m$^2$ in 2D, and $0.1260$ m$^3$ in 3D. These values describe the same local distance scale and threshold, rather than fields with the same numerical size in different units. More generally, the geometry and moment formula simplify to $\mu_s\simeq(\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma/q)^d$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p168 sha256 c40a9c218e56b8642dce4369db75a188c704723b435ffeda01cd87fb8810f502 -->
p168, lines 168-168; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p166; next: p172

> This approximation has a measurable error even for a smooth Gaussian process. At $q=1.1,\sigma=2.4$ m, its 1D mean is $5.469$ m, compared with the [exact stationary crossing result](ch-place-fields.html#sec-place-inference) $3.746$ m, an overestimate of $46.0\%$. At $q=5$ the error is $3.73\%$. [This is why the paper compares data to finite threshold simulations as well as to limiting curves.]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p172 sha256 ff864c1077a2589498fd3bf05187ff3d7e4d9a4009d33a0cfb023d7fb36e0d27 -->
p172, lines 172-172; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p168; next: p174

> The printed supplement appears to contain three algebraic errors in this calculation:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p174 sha256 916af41df8e7a07fcadee25de0f885c71ee63808c3a11e063878ac3469d10f1b -->
p174, lines 174-174; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p172; next: p175

> Equation S18 prints multiplication by mean size where the preceding moment formula requires division.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p175 sha256 cbdcb17a297de8911bad66e90999d69ea4f98394709189fef43cfa0b609e7be5 -->
p175, lines 175-175; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p174; next: p176

> Equation S23 omits a factor $\sqrt2$ when solving the parabola in S22 for full width.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p176 sha256 6e39c1527f2b69e4322110f808587d200d1f28916006e58fe58cf3424b5662ba -->
p176, lines 176-176; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p175; next: p178

> In S25 the finite-height quadratic exponent should be negative.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p178 sha256 afeb2d1b47a2c8122dee33aba308b6231e92c7619d3dfe22ca505f0e2cae403c -->
p178, lines 178-178; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p176; next: p184

> The derivation above retains the main paper's distributional form and obtains the constants from the displayed parabola, the moment integral, and the independent crossing calculation. These corrections concern the printed derivation; they do not establish that the simulation code or empirical fits used the same mistakes.[^p3-corrections]

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md::sec:PLACE-SLICES -->

### Slices test spatial structure beyond field volume

Location: line 182, anchor `sec:PLACE-SLICES`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 184
Closing passage line: 214

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p184 sha256 f7a4d1df563695a6f456b6601656f1ad5be30d37c443f44aea6f10e8f88cc427 -->
p184, lines 184-184; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p178; next: p186

> A model can fit field volumes and still place activity incorrectly within those volumes. Slicing the map tests that internal spatial structure.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p186 sha256 7fcf2dfddf9b5536a5156f479958372adf7f807b8bf2cec9a245d5fda588393c -->
p186, lines 186-186; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p184; next: p188

> Restrict a Gaussian field to a straight line $x=x_0+tv$. At any chosen values $t_1,\ldots,t_n$, its values are a subset of the original jointly Gaussian values. They therefore remain jointly Gaussian. Along the line the covariance is $C((t-t')v)$; for a unit direction in an isotropic field it has the same local length scale $\sigma$. The same argument applies to a flat plane. First slicing and then thresholding gives the same active portions as thresholding and then slicing.[^p3-slices-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p188 sha256 92a306ecd5eec60105b9f9987ed8da5e18e423dd44d473ac381bb4733ac32c70 -->
p188, lines 188-188; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p186; next: p190

> Thus a line through a 2D or 3D map has the one dimensional excursion prediction, and a plane through a 3D map has the two dimensional prediction. The field being counted is now a connected active interval or patch in that slice. One 3D component can be missed, cut into multiple pieces, or intersect several slices.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p190 sha256 8288d06f0fd3bc7d03fe0a33a62b0c92937d4d02dd69a3155ac75dc3cf9f1a7d -->
p190, lines 190-190; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p188; next: p192

> The sampling rule matters. Choosing one original field uniformly and cutting through its centre is different from choosing lines uniformly through space. Large fields are more likely to be hit by a spatially sampled line, and most hits pass away from their centres. The Gaussian restriction argument describes excursions along the sampled line. The radius distribution for fields counted once each cannot be used directly as the distribution of lengths observed along a line. In the high peak picture, an isotropic $d$ dimensional field has a radius of order $\sigma/q$; converting this same radius into $d$ dimensional size and then cutting it requires both hit probability and cut position.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p192 sha256 dcbb4960853c0a7ca89e7d923d8f592935f10cada772e0a1560dac9b68666ed7 -->
p192, lines 192-192; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p190; next: p194

> Figure 2 separates the measurements by spatial dimension:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p194 sha256 1f9b91b1e977a0b284df50042941b94319781a39609cb7614e1d497d44f6d3c4 -->
p194, lines 194-194; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p192; next: p195

> Panels A–B illustrate 2D thresholding. C measures lengths along slices, D measures original field areas, and E counts fields per observed cell.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p195 sha256 fe81365d724dec1c56d7d5d27277e05053bbf816b7b1fd4e5a0d6ee7b616b8ec -->
p195, lines 195-195; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p194; next: p197

> F–G show 3D examples; H, I, and J compare line lengths, planar areas, and full volumes.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p197 sha256 3115561938754120eb755ebd8926180531d2b37e09a2d10e2898eb52b8919acd -->
p197, lines 197-199; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p195; next: p203

> <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure"> <summary>Original paper figure: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025), Figure 2</summary> <div class="publisher-fold-body">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p203 sha256 fcd1e43c0c40c2d1dd2932edba0594c38927752553522ce5ed959562b6a78f49 -->
p203, lines 203-204; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p197; next: p206

> </div> </details>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p206 sha256 51306bbd9103429e5496bfcc9d2f209c1bb18f39d51be7e4e092e23e4315cdc0 -->
p206, lines 206-206; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p203; next: p208

> [The dashed analytical curves change with the dimension of the measurement, even within one recorded map. The alternative spherical-field model in Figure S2 matches empirical volumes and peak rates by construction but fails these slice distributions and the topology test. Matching volumes alone is therefore insufficient.]{.key-statement}[^p3-slices-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p208 sha256 3b8d83374dfb9d52ebac9f126f11458d071ea38d11fbc51f6811c0e32025738c -->

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05b-place-sizes.md::sec:PLACE-SLOPES -->

### Why slopes at field boundaries are Rayleigh

Location: line 217, anchor `sec:PLACE-SLOPES`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 219
Closing passage line: 251

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p219 sha256 e344c1bebf0db137fc241848261e512767ee4d9a2a050e602e340fdef310b0a0 -->
p219, lines 219-219; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p214; next: p221

> The derivative at an arbitrary position is Gaussian. A field boundary is a selected position, however, and selecting it changes the derivative distribution.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p221 sha256 fb4d93baeb2d07a0468341c3f611f160bb689ff9747952eaaad5691a739b2303 -->
p221, lines 221-221; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p219; next: p229

> Return to the [crossing integral](ch-place-fields.html#sec-place-kac-rice) and retain only slopes between $v$ and $v+dv$. Their contribution is proportional to $v p_{z'}(v)dv$. Dividing by the total contribution of positive slopes gives the slope density at a typical upcrossing:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p229 sha256 f1f910eb66c77377f6dabc079779bf3515a162a53b55552782afbca24cb90310 -->
p229, lines 229-229; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p221; next: p233

> The height density cancels because height and slope are independent. [This Rayleigh law is therefore independent of $q$ under the smooth stationary Gaussian model, without a high threshold approximation.]{.key-statement} Absolute slopes at downward boundaries have the same law. The rate slope is $av$, so its scale also depends on the firing rate gain. This selection effect explains why a half Gaussian law for a positive derivative sampled uniformly in space would be wrong at crossings.[^p3-slope-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p233 sha256 65eb0b476fa28ef5ac949160ef950c255718a10344956fe5e81ac9a5dd55fc0a -->
p233, lines 233-233; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p229; next: p239

> There is also an independent check on the high threshold width constant. Starting at an upcrossing with slope $v$, the local excursion is approximately

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p239 sha256 8a1dc3346431d47dcdafec9e28737546824f0fb1d2eb9dfcbcabc9771538b7a2 -->
p239, lines 239-239; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p233; next: p243

> Its second root is $s=2\sigma^2v/q$. Changing variables in the exact crossing slope law gives $p(s)=2\beta s e^{-\beta s^2}$ with $\beta=q^2/(8\sigma^2)$, the same constant as the peak calculation for $v_1=2$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p243 sha256 594efec82ac884dd9d09a6353fa67727a6b437fd2fe0866144d93a9a8f14f3c9 -->
p243, lines 243-243; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p239; next: p245

> Figure 3A identifies which width, peak, and slopes are measured. B compares local peak counts, C–D compare rate and size, and E compares boundary slopes. The paper measures boundary slopes by a finite difference between samples 0.1 m apart. This approximates a derivative and has its own spatial resolution. It is not a direct observation of the underlying synaptic input.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p245 sha256 7dd8a49d1fc85b0197ccf6145d2da2ee11e06a3e5e746bfc6a03b9a2cd22a5c5 -->
p245, lines 245-247; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p243; next: p251

> <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure"> <summary>Original paper figure: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025), Figure 3</summary> <div class="publisher-fold-body">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p251 sha256 fcd1e43c0c40c2d1dd2932edba0594c38927752553522ce5ed959562b6a78f49 -->
p251, lines 251-252; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p245; next: none

> </div> </details>

## ch:PLACE-TESTS - Holes and measurements test the spatial explanation

Source file: `content/chapters/05c-place-tests.md`

Purpose: Recordings show where a hippocampal cell is active as an animal moves through an environment. This chapter tests the spatial model against the shapes and arrangement of those regions, then asks what agreement can reveal about the inputs and connections that produce them.

Prerequisites: ch:PLACE-FIELDS, ch:PLACE-SIZES

Sidecar sections: Holes distinguish maps with the same field count, What was fitted and what was tested, Random input weights can generate Gaussian maps

Blocks: 6 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p3 sha256 10277ca2ab6f8e67f23255aef2046645261e23271f5ea37e115baaab490a5036 -->
p3, lines 3-3; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p5

> [Two maps can have the same field sizes and counts while arranging their active regions differently. One may contain rings or tunnels that the other lacks. Can the Gaussian spatial model account for these differences, and how much would that agreement tell us about the circuit that produced the maps?]{.key-statement}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p5 sha256 2e3309c33081b2eaac4e0945b5a0ed403df1d85e2d55ca39616ed4ab8c71551c -->
p5, lines 5-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p7

> We first count connected regions and holes together, which gives a prediction across activity thresholds. The boundary of the observed environment matters for this calculation, so we keep its contributions. We then examine which measured quantities set the model's parameters and which provide further tests. Finally, a sum of many randomly weighted inputs shows one way a circuit could produce Gaussian spatial input, while making clear which assumptions and circuit measurements that explanation still needs.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 5bb1f6982a8d2f312b9d5b8748fe3ca08dcf18f2c5923f2091b0926d6e7be8f2 -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p11

> The map is still fixed over space. As before, $q$ is the normalized activity threshold, $\sigma$ is the local spatial scale, and $a$ converts normalized input above threshold into firing rate. The number of spatial dimensions is $d$; it is separate from the diffusion coefficient $D$ used in the memory chapters.

### Holes distinguish maps with the same field count

Location: line 9, anchor `sec:PLACE-TOPOLOGY`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 11
Closing passage line: 117

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p11 sha256 dcc3b4afcb6573eacb3352443986df40586f55ef693a5fc13d5bea28cd0d3ade -->
p11, lines 11-11; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p7; next: p13

> Two maps can have the same field count and total active area but different holes. As the activity threshold rises, active regions shrink and can split or disappear. The numbers of components and holes can change.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p13 sha256 0c2ab7e80f3619baf767872884b081ee44ec7f72e71df3bb2e4ec839a1725abb -->
p13, lines 13-13; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p11; next: p15

> [The [**Euler characteristic**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, written $\chi$, combines these counts differently in each dimension:]{.definition-explanation}

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p15 sha256 6493b64c127b0011b545370586936c17f7222669fa417de7cf73ec92c070655d -->
p15, lines 15-15; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p13; next: p16

> In 1D it equals the number of connected active intervals.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p16 sha256 e80754190f1e0c1656f81f4735a2b540833f465734dc911c120a55ba6d010d3c -->
p16, lines 16-16; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p15; next: p17

> In 2D it is the number of connected components minus the number of independent holes. Three disjoint disks have $\chi=3$; one connected ring has $\chi=1-1=0$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p17 sha256 f6c9a4588ef99b3239033d5ecc56a4ee4f69ab2872575aad354694ebdd772e19 -->
p17, lines 17-17; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p16; next: p19

> In 3D it is components minus independent tunnels plus enclosed cavities. A solid ball has $\chi=1$, a solid ring has $\chi=0$, and a hollow spherical shell has $\chi=2$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p19 sha256 17b85e25c1216cbb4b2f7910697ec49a04db8157c0b0dd6361ff5955092006a8 -->
p19, lines 19-19; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p17; next: p21

> [These counts are the first three [**Betti numbers**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}: numbers of independent components, loops, and cavities, respectively.]{.definition-explanation}[^p3-ec-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p21 sha256 f0d912c242f1c3298afc120785c32067c289e55b31ec386020154b4944f6df02 -->
p21, lines 21-21; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p19; next: p23

> Thresholding one fixed smooth map separates the number of connected fields from the number of holes.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p23 sha256 2c643bd85d3e8933fd7127e586cd0d302d74dcd69d691fc3921ef217cc019e39 -->
p23, lines 23-23; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p21; next: p27

> <div class="figure-scroll">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p27 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p27, lines 27-27; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p23; next: p29

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p29 sha256 be79eb69f6109d899f86e8bac03bee74dbf19681aa49cf26f8bc258eee48b58a -->
p29, lines 29-29; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p27; next: p31

> A negative chi means that holes outnumber components in this 2D example. It does not mean a negative number of fields. When holes are absent, chi equals the component count.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p31 sha256 7dfe629d22b7ae95521c9a32476afdb57ff0bce877799536871474f12f56ad16 -->
p31, lines 31-31; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p29; next: p33

> The expected Euler characteristic has an exact Gaussian formula even where identifying it with field count would be wrong. We already derived the 1D case. To understand the higher dimensional factors, we use a mathematical result: [for a smooth function on a compact domain whose [**critical point**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}s have zero gradient and invertible Hessians, the Euler characteristic of a level set region can be computed as a signed count of critical points, including boundary contributions. This is the relevant conclusion of [**Morse theory**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}.]{.definition-explanation} We do not prove that topological theorem here. The calculation below shows how its Gaussian expectation produces the paper's polynomials and boundary terms; the general Gaussian result is given by Taylor's kinematic formula.[^p3-taylor]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p33 sha256 9d413cbb462a8805e6f97d941c734d4c3a1a0e7be354b3687908cb6fe05e8c05 -->
p33, lines 33-33; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p31; next: p41

> First take the interior of a $j$ dimensional region, with $j\le3$, and set $\lambda=\sigma^{-2}$. A critical point has gradient zero. The multidimensional Kac–Rice rule counts these zeros with the absolute Hessian determinant; the Morse signs replace that absolute determinant by $\det(-H)$. The resulting signed density above level $q$ is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p41 sha256 a54adb9dc66647dc11a47e1a99103408b5b1516b6a0959b97f26244ec1647232 -->
p41, lines 41-41; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p33; next: p43

> where $\phi(b)=e^{-b^2/2}/\sqrt{2\pi}$. The gradient's $j$ components are independent Gaussians of variance $\lambda$, so $p_{\nabla z}(0)=(2\pi\lambda)^{-j/2}$. The determinant measures how spatial volume near a gradient zero converts to gradient volume, just as the slope did for a scalar crossing.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p43 sha256 710dd87df9b25e8475fe88f2037ff2191ed664f4e4b000fe059c857b143b6732 -->
p43, lines 43-43; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p41; next: p51

> The resulting signed densities are

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p51 sha256 a7b5eaf9118abeb9aa49bb2332a925d2d12b0f8fadb10cec749495df1f5cc4a1 -->
p51, lines 51-51; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p43; next: p55

> These are signed densities per length, area, and volume, not three formulas for positive field counts.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p55 sha256 a43eb99f7072b08aea13f4bddee0b295009d95289a7d528c6b5cc3653298ebc9 -->
p55, lines 55-55; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p51; next: p63

> Here are the Gaussian algebra steps behind the determinant average. Write the conditional Hessian as $H=-b\lambda I+G$, where $G$ has zero mean. Differentiating an isotropic covariance four times gives, for distinct indices $i,k$,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p63 sha256 611e89c52f18e27cbd5d4f4abe1873b2e7174ca8a18394cf1b65f67d44e707a4 -->
p63, lines 63-63; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p55; next: p69

> The constant $\kappa$ describes a fourth derivative of covariance. Conditioning on height subtracts $\lambda^2$ from the covariance of the two diagonal entries, but not from the off-diagonal variance. Hence each pair contributes

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p69 sha256 6eeec516802772ad652d966ac77d65a1e4ea637da1f35a5fe49eb8ff995f112f -->
p69, lines 69-69; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p63; next: p71

> For $j=1$, $\mathbb E[\det(-H)\mid b]=b\lambda$. For $j=2$, expand the two by two determinant to obtain $\lambda^2(b^2-1)$. For $j=3$, the leading term is $b^3\lambda^3$; the three index pairs each contribute $-b\lambda^3$, and all odd centered Gaussian products vanish. The result is $\lambda^3(b^3-3b)$. The unknown fourth derivative $\kappa$ has canceled.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p71 sha256 ccb3f22095106d1560d4d4abd636f04939cba9b69067374fb410037ad38bb37d -->
p71, lines 71-71; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p69; next: p81

> Integrating the three polynomials gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p81 sha256 1932d4d5337ab09c2c8efb724627a22c5f4745d76b0c90944dba46389d9ef93b -->
p81, lines 81-81; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p71; next: p85

> For example, differentiate $-q\phi(q)$ to get $(q^2-1)\phi(q)$, which verifies the second integral using its vanishing upper endpoint. Combining the determinant, gradient density, and integral gives the signed densities above.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p85 sha256 2c20f8c2c868fed491d30dc311931c8e5599abc6b9fa53e204f7213a6e3121d8 -->
p85, lines 85-85; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p81; next: p98

> Now include the boundary of an ordinary closed square or cube of side $L$, whose opposite faces are not joined and with every intersecting component retained. On a $j$ dimensional face, derivatives along the face give the same calculation as above. Each of its $d-j$ gradient components perpendicular to the face and directed out of the box must have the appropriate sign to contribute to the boundary Morse count. Symmetry and independence give a factor $2^{-(d-j)}$. There are $2^{d-j}\binom dj$ such faces, each of size $L^j$. Their combined coefficient is therefore $\binom djL^j$. Corners supply $Q(q)$ in total. With $r=L/\sigma$, the formulas become

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p98 sha256 326eb6657b7e2ac143f23713a457ffa350de85acbf14c87f48af0b7096e91aab -->
p98, lines 98-98; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p85; next: p100

> These reproduce the paper's Equations 8–10 for interval, square, and cube geometry. They are not the formulas for a periodic box. For a rectangle or rectangular room, replace $2L$ or $3L$ by the sum of side lengths, $3L^2$ by the sum of pairwise side products, and $L^3$ by volume. A general domain requires its own geometric boundary factors. Applying only the volume term to a small room would discard real boundary contributions.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p100 sha256 693938cc06386ff5efcb5591233f271e8708277170c58071134b6cf32d2cb7d3 -->
p100, lines 100-100; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p98; next: p102

> The formulas have two limiting checks:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p102 sha256 a15145057eb3ab131b21ae3a54664e9a7558d828c740087ceaaceb4bc74a702c -->
p102, lines 102-102; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p100; next: p103

> As $q\to-\infty$, the whole box becomes active and all three expectations tend to one.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p103 sha256 33bb49f8aa1b54e50dfb140b4f9fea863a54fb803b3a695f47796c8010f47d59 -->
p103, lines 103-103; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p102; next: p105

> As $q\to+\infty$, they tend to zero.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p105 sha256 1e79b0b04b033c2327f0604cd81864e15fabe4744aa9bf378e6246102c0aef42 -->
p105, lines 105-105; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p103; next: p107

> At intermediate thresholds the higher dimensional signed densities can be negative because tunnels or holes outnumber components. For example a cube with $L/\sigma=10$ has expected $\chi=-20.06$ at $q=0$. A negative expected Euler characteristic is meaningful; a negative expected field count would not be.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p107 sha256 b19a80efc6ef3cebb84771df7a93a897523e424ec20f0935d3698bb0248e429e -->
p107, lines 107-107; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p105; next: p115

> At a sufficiently high threshold, almost every component is a small solid blob with one peak and no holes. Then $\chi\simeq N$. For a large box, retaining the leading volume term and the leading power of $q$ gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p115 sha256 3e99e169f76da7d6308d00454de6b147ec2d3c69aa0157b1402b9dfe2afce696 -->
p115, lines 115-115; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p107; next: p117

> where $V$ is environment length, area, or volume. Dividing $VQ(q)$ by this count and using $Q(q)\simeq\phi(q)/q$ recovers $\mu_s\simeq(\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma/q)^d$, independently checking the size-law mean. [The exact EC formula and this approximate component count have different ranges of validity.]{.key-statement}[^p3-higher-count]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p117 sha256 378192a1076eb211507796aa3a77b10ceff0b27946bf47939d54b587c90b9f7b -->
p117, lines 117-117; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p115; next: p121

> Read Figure 3F–H as whole curves, rather than single field counts. Increasing the applied firing rate cutoff by $t\ge0$ changes the normalized level to $q+t/a$. It can reveal separation between peaks or remove components. The measured maps only test levels at or above their original effective cutoff; the rest of an analytic curve is a prediction. The gain was matched using mean maximum firing rate before the EC comparison, so the horizontal conversion from Hz to normalized level also uses fitted information. Two dimensional holes and three dimensional tunnels help explain the nonmonotonic curves.[^p3-ec-source]

For the concrete-wording field, use this form: line N; phrase: ...; paraphrase: ...; actor: ...; object: ...; action: ...; condition or consequence: ...; verdict: retain, revise, or delete; replacement or retention reason: ....
Context review must explain the literal meaning, prerequisites, analogy limits when relevant, and reason for appearing here. If no correction is needed, give a specific retention reason; lint success is not evidence.
For System setup and component relationships, cite the passage and fill: setting: ...; roles: ...; objects and flow: ...; assumptions: ...; verdict: ....
For Relevant alternatives, common criteria, and selection or scope, cite the passage and fill: common problem: ...; options: ...; relationship: ...; criteria: ...; selection or scope: ...; verdict: ....
For Essential explanation beyond the checklist, cite the passage and fill: reader task: ...; dependencies checked: ...; reasoning chain: ...; unresolved: ....
For Expert objection, cite the passage and fill: objection: ...; kind: ...; target: ...; disposition: ...; reason: ....
For system setup and alternatives, an adequate review uses verdict: adequate. Where inapplicable, use Not applicable: line N; reason: <specific reader-benefit reason>.
Expert objection cannot be marked Not applicable. Write the strongest correction a domain expert would make to this unit's text or figures; set kind to correctness or wording; name its target passage or figure label; set disposition to fixed or does not apply; give the reason. An objection of none is not a review. The strongest correction is the one that would change what a reader believes or does. Aim first at what the unit claims: a promise word such as always, once, ensures or remains that is false or needs a condition; a named method described wrongly; code that does not do what the text says; a table cell; a diagnostic sequence that skips a stage; a verb without an actor; a label naming something other than what is drawn; a figure that disagrees with the text or another figure; and example content found in neither the chapter nor its sources. A unit whose claim-bearing content includes code, a table, an equation, or a figure needs a correctness objection; a wording objection alone does not count there.
Claims to check lists the unit's statements most likely to make checkable claims: every table row and every figure or table caption with its alt text, and each sentence with a promise word, a quantity or calculation, code font, a citation, or a section or figure reference. Footnote definitions are left to the citation checks. When packets compare with a revision, every changed statement is listed first and marked changed. For each claim, set verdict to true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim, and after checked: say how it was checked, such as the code test, the recomputation, or the source and its location. The list is a floor, not the extent of the review.
For each code listing, write test: followed by the path, relative to the guide, of a test that runs it on the inputs the text makes claims about, or not run: followed by the reason and how its claims were checked instead.
Essential explanation cannot be marked Not applicable. Trace the reader's task and its dependencies, including gaps not covered by other fields. Record unresolved: none only after resolving essential gaps; otherwise describe them and keep the unit verdict open. A complete checklist is not proof of complete teaching.

### What was fitted and what was tested

Location: line 119, anchor `sec:PLACE-DATA`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 121
Closing passage line: 195

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p121 sha256 fcc7333ac30c95d0624d811447fbe4a23eb853252323fcd377d119206443ec5b -->
p121, lines 121-121; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p117; next: p123

> The experiments supply estimated firing rate maps, not the hidden Gaussian input. To assess a prediction, we must therefore track both which summaries set its parameters and which processing decisions change the map's geometry.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p123 sha256 6fc88a9a6f9d269e8bf8bdf952dd12345dcd732a864a643ac2dbf710f2953bc1 -->
p123, lines 123-123; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p121; next: p125

> The paper reanalyzes earlier bat and rodent recordings. It uses different available summaries in different conditions. The inspected article and supplement do not clearly give the final included cell count for every analysis, particularly the long-tunnel and 3D reanalyses; counts from an original recording study should therefore not be silently substituted for those final samples.[^p3-processing]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p125 sha256 9960784fd6b96a12fbf5b8855619bf1a26dae079bce219c3b96e0c64ec075ad2 -->
p125, lines 125-125; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p123; next: p129

> <div class="table-responsive">

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p129 sha256 aac32651b10f567c461b9b4f255d6fb1fa6859b5368d8bd9a51af920ab21cf23 -->
p129, lines 129-129; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p125; next: p131

> </div>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p131 sha256 cf08531dee7b08e4bf8a41ab1a4f001748c1e62c06748bde0ed65a0690be08a8 -->
p131, lines 131-131; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p129; next: p133

> The STAR Methods call the virtual-track animals rats in one sentence; Figure 1F and the main results identify them as mice. Several STAR figure cross-references also appear displaced. The table follows the main figure captions and the analyses described in full, rather than those isolated labels.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p133 sha256 8e3fde9de76be6e2510db7f3f8b28ea17c399c247375cf85c9be6ca80ec5cd32 -->
p133, lines 133-133; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p131; next: p135

> Figure 1C distinguishes a limiting analytical Rayleigh curve from the finite threshold simulation histogram. Its parameters were constrained by both mean length and mean gap, so the full length histogram is an additional check. Figure 1D does the same for gaps. E tests skew and excess kurtosis of log lengths. For $Y=\log s$, write $m_k=\mathbb E[(Y-\mathbb EY)^k]$; [then [**skew**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is $m_3/m_2^{3/2}$ and [**excess kurtosis**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is $m_4/m_2^2-3$. A [**lognormal distribution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} of sizes makes $Y$ Gaussian and both population quantities zero.]{.definition-explanation} Finite sample estimates still fluctuate, which is why the figure compares simulated sampling distributions.[^p3-fit-tests]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p135 sha256 b1f553f2639a97b1586b6cece82d42b99986bf191d16e57a7c9e2d233cba6e13 -->
p135, lines 135-135; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p133; next: p137

> For the long-tunnel data, the authors compared the Gaussian excursion model with a fitted lognormal using likelihoods and criteria that penalize fitted parameter count. Those comparisons favored the Gaussian model. [These are in-sample model comparisons. They are not reported held-out forecasts.]{.key-statement} Model histogram error bars come from $10^4$ simulations matched to the number of recorded cells; they do not quantify every source of animal-level or measurement uncertainty.[^p3-fit-tests]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p137 sha256 fe31888b99a4ce10647967042fa2c584ddae08f10e0b505108b54cececbfa1f2 -->
p137, lines 137-137; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p135; next: p139

> The processing choices affect the quantities being compared:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p139 sha256 f91928adb1e4464c51ab809d677c77f844454f30f2fdaa966298ace011e3880d -->
p139, lines 139-139; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p137; next: p140

> Bat maps were rectified at about 0.5 Hz for denoising.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p140 sha256 7de3da49d6349630d9874544997482f868cde5da2ed0f7c24a6bde0c929e183f -->
p140, lines 140-140; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p139; next: p141

> The 3D maps, sampled in 10 cm bins, also received a median filter.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p141 sha256 799cd4ed4f964c04fefb6515114b81a8eab3bde434a75485f278f1e3beb6affc -->
p141, lines 141-141; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p140; next: p142

> Fields larger than three standard deviations above mean 3D volume were removed as possible merges, including from slice analyses.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p142 sha256 9dbde3764418e376c57c6ff9c913244d759b68de23b18244355b472cb37f2c00 -->
p142, lines 142-142; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p141; next: p143

> Peak counting excluded incomplete boundary fields and used a range of smoothing widths.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p143 sha256 357d7334617df0f85e9c84a7f5be441e41edb8856bc4f30ef6b49265ec8b7a9a -->
p143, lines 143-143; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p142; next: p145

> The 1D rate versus width comparison used an additional 1 Hz cutoff.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p145 sha256 1845eacb0715739262475909b324316dc28f7e1c4463bc2a559493d379564f06 -->
p145, lines 145-145; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p143; next: p147

> [These decisions can remove small components, alter peaks, and change tails. They belong to the definition of the analyzed fields.]{.key-statement}[^p3-processing]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p147 sha256 dcb50d8cf6f679bb583bf02f9ffcda304dcb196885df8b4fc070402780a20741 -->
p147, lines 147-147; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p145; next: p149

> The supplement adds useful checks. Figure S1 matches the rat maze's 15 cm minimum length cutoff in simulations and removes unusually large empirical fields; changing that cutoff changes log-length moments. Figure S5 shows sensitivity of the higher-moment comparison across small denoising thresholds, while the rate versus width slope becomes stable above about 1 Hz. Figure S2 compares the spherical-field alternative and measures boundary curvature. [Locally, [**mean curvature**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} averages bending in two principal directions and [**Gaussian curvature**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} multiplies them: a spherical patch bends the same way in both directions, while a saddle bends in opposite directions.]{.definition-explanation} These are extra shape measurements; they are not needed to derive the field-size law. Surface representations and finite differences affect their estimates.[^p3-supp-tests]

p149, lines 149-151; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p147; next: p155

p164, lines 164-165; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p158; next: p167

> </div> </details>

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p167 sha256 f3ab73148350b61d76a33097435dc578a9f1ca338b1d4d18ec526535397d61ca -->
p167, lines 167-167; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p164; next: p169

> Figure 4A–B summarizes fitted parameter changes across a small set of environments. The correlation length increases with environment size while the threshold generally decreases. Increasing $\sigma$ reduces crossings per metre; decreasing $q$ increases them. Both parameters also affect mean length, so these effects must be considered together. Figure S3 reports count scaling exponents below one and hence a weak decline in count per unit environment size. These cross-experiment trends do not establish a biological scaling law. In particular, the 3D table's reported $\sigma=4.0$ has an unclear distance-unit conversion relative to the spatial binning, so it should not be read as a verified value in metres.[^p3-trends]

p169, lines 169-171; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p167; next: p175

p175, lines 175-176; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p169; next: p178

p178, lines 178-180; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p175; next: p184

p184, lines 184-185; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p178; next: p187

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p187 sha256 b55c4804307932ad51ca9f7cae0073f9ba356aad17cd6b5853035339596dd39c -->
p187, lines 187-187; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p184; next: p189

> The model also allows differences between cells. Figure S4 draws a positive threshold separately for each cell and keeps that threshold for two otherwise independent maps representing opposite flight directions. Low-threshold cells tend to have more and larger fields in both maps; this shared cell property creates correlations between flight directions even when field locations differ between directions. Two distribution parameters were chosen to match the two empirical correlations, so those correlations are fitted features of this extension. Its continued agreement with length and gap distributions is the additional comparison. This extension should not be described as a two-parameter model shared by all cells.[^p3-threshold-variation]

### Random input weights can generate Gaussian maps

Location: line 198, anchor `sec:PLACE-WIRING`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 200
Closing passage line: 251

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p200 sha256 30e3871f3bad882f606126c235a5966435172ad279aeb11adc8329e5945ee265 -->
p200, lines 200-200; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p195; next: p202

> The statistical model becomes biologically useful if it suggests how a circuit could make the maps. A random weighted sum of many inputs is one possible mechanism, and the supplement makes its assumptions explicit.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p202 sha256 ab2036762a39be9d87e142db79ff937001d5d7c0fbdea0a4f66e9c082cc9effa -->
p202, lines 202-202; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p200; next: p204

> At position $x$, each input cell contributes according to its spatial firing profile. Fixed connection weights determine how those contributions combine at the receiving cell. The source treats the sum as effective input relative to a background that can be balanced through inhibition or regulation of excitation and inhibition. The signed random weights below are part of this simplified input model; they do not establish the signs of individual biological synapses.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p204 sha256 e1f790cefb655302fbd37c34a3be0616c73966e518fa76fad4af5f09b43a2753 -->
p204, lines 204-204; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p202; next: p210

> Let $u_i(x)$ be the fixed spatial tuning of input neuron $i$, and let independent weights $W_i$ have zero mean and variance $v_W$. The profiles $u_i$ may have nonzero spatial means. The expectation below is over weights: their zero means make the summed input $h(x)$ have mean zero. The summed input is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p210 sha256 f03c2fb286e932a8abde3a5d115daa53c64ad5be34e90bdb9110f157aee7d49f -->
p210, lines 210-210; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p204; next: p212

> [At a finite list of positions this is a sum of independent random vectors, one per input cell. Under a multivariate central limit condition, with a suitable normalization and no single input dominating, the sum approaches a Gaussian vector as the number of contributing inputs grows. Applying this at every finite list supplies the Gaussian-process description. The theorem needs many contributing inputs at the positions in question; a large anatomical input count alone does not show that this condition holds.]{.key-statement}[^p3-wiring-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p212 sha256 d2a002d6434381ff1dc22fa84811a52d1e42a1ec251760afaadf1cda16ded894 -->
p212, lines 212-212; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p210; next: p222

> The covariance calculation exposes which assumption supplies spatial structure:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p222 sha256 c4eb7325fa7f0d49084503224892c66aac55a440dbd2635241e43c433c8f9c5d -->
p222, lines 222-222; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p212; next: p224

> The same fixed weight multiplies an input cell's activity at both positions. For positive input profiles, cells active at both $x$ and $y$ therefore make positive contributions to covariance across possible weight choices. Little overlap gives a smaller contribution. For profiles defined as deviations from background, the products can also be negative. Broad input profiles can produce broad spatial covariance, as the Gaussian example below shows.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p224 sha256 597d32eb83bc21fe4c797636463d0e5fd28d6b21905a2fad133f199afeef9d36 -->
p224, lines 224-224; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p222; next: p230

> If inputs are translated copies $u_i(x)=u_0(x-x_i)$ with centres densely and uniformly covering space at density $\rho$, the sum is approximately

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p230 sha256 ebb579e0fc866a1f90ab68176d44e64d032b011e4debdfddd14b2dc7fc6e5a13 -->
p230, lines 230-230; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p224; next: p232

> The assumptions supply different parts of the model:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p232 sha256 90c27326ab42b5537c52010bf559cd2ff3643ffbab33375d768e984eb660729a -->
p232, lines 232-232; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p230; next: p233

> Uniform coverage supplies stationarity away from boundaries.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p233 sha256 7153af7d68d9a6d40b69b03df0baf14d95b7ab089d10a1e4f6355679a6e62844 -->
p233, lines 233-233; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p232; next: p234

> Smooth input tuning supplies smoothness.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p234 sha256 d6d431ae6dbbfd92402270596e783b84f6b03e75903f9e87c6c59e7dc9d32d0b -->
p234, lines 234-234; list_item; priority: unchanged; previous: p233; next: p236

> Random weights supply the Gaussian limit under their independence and size conditions.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p236 sha256 d05f1c44bcdc894deae5496fcd9bdf4608fd0bb389533b73165de5bef5865130 -->
p236, lines 236-236; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p234; next: p238

> For input profiles with different shapes, average the last integral over the distribution of those shapes, as in Methods S2, Equation S8.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p238 sha256 1802ca0bd310d2485430f44358eec676a43663fac05d55c5c9bec5a38d82b9b8 -->
p238, lines 238-238; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p236; next: p240

> At fixed profile shape, increasing the weight variance $v_W$ or input density $\rho$ increases the covariance amplitude. It does not change the normalized spatial dependence in this construction. Widening each input profile instead broadens the distances over which positions share input. These are different changes: input amplitude can alter the effective threshold $q$ when the absolute threshold is held fixed, whereas input width changes the spatial scale.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p240 sha256 5ec0a727a94019dc93bbc6230121b363e41d23c719b1332f15161be79971b08a -->
p240, lines 240-240; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p238; next: p247

> For example, take $u_0(s)=R_0e^{-s^2/(2b^2)}$ in one dimension. Completing the square gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p247 sha256 98b28bbeb7835ea3e4c956ee74d5e7ca58b3c878e8246b85f50b22fdc3763114 -->
p247, lines 247-247; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p240; next: p249

> The covariance's Gaussian standard deviation is $\sqrt2 b$, fixing the width convention used in the comparison.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p249 sha256 e835b9977f7aed2f3e810f609834c0207090ed4589a1870f20eacd948c6210d8 -->
p249, lines 249-249; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p247; next: p251

> Agreement with the Gaussian field predictions supports this random-summation account as a possible explanation. It does not uniquely recover synaptic weights from rate maps. Correlated inputs, a structured component added to many random contributions, plasticity, or recurrent processing could also produce approximately Gaussian spatial input under some conditions. The paper measures rate maps, rather than the relevant weights or subthreshold input directly. Its analyzed environments were familiar and fairly uniform; stationarity may fail near landmarks, boundaries, rewards, or in changing tasks.[^p3-discussion]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p251 sha256 8f0fa7f518ecf4e1c1e5a343cbfe1df99c431550b705147f661dfb7bbf13384d -->
p251, lines 251-251; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p249; next: none

> [The result to carry into the synthesis is therefore specific: thresholding a smooth spatial Gaussian field links field arrangement, shape, and topology through a small set of parameters and gives several successful cross-checks. Turning that statistical agreement into a claim about circuit mechanism requires additional measurements or perturbations.]{.key-statement}

## ch:SYNTHESIS - Spatial maps and memory dynamics need separate tests

Source file: `content/chapters/06-synthesis.md`

Purpose: The three papers address different links between nerve-cell activity and behavior: retaining a value, producing eye movement, and representing location. This chapter compares what the models and recordings establish, and separates supported connections from further questions about how a complete neural circuit would work.

Prerequisites: ch:MEMORY-READOUT, ch:EYE-CIRCUIT, ch:PLACE-TESTS

Sidecar sections: The three papers constrain maps, dynamics, and movement, How a static map can carry a moving estimate, Example: opposite rate changes from the same position error, Each model needs a test matched to its claim, Related studies change assumptions and leave circuit choices open, A joint test would compare tuning maps with population fluctuations

Blocks: 1 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected
